Shock profiles for the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation with hard potentials
Dominic Wynter

Abstract
. The Boltzmann equation models gas dynamics in the low density or high Mach number regime, using a statistical description of molecular interactions. Planar shock wave solutions have been constructed for the Boltzmann equation for hard potentials with angular cutoff, and more recently for the Landau equation of plasma dynamics. In this work, we construct shock profile solutions for the Boltzmann equation where the molecular interactions are long-range, and we show these solutions to be smooth and well approximated by compressible Navier Stokes shock profiles. Our proof procedes by standard energy estimates and a quantitative Chapman-Enskog approximation.



1.
Introduction

Shock wave phenomena are of great importance in the theory of gas dynamics, and can be modelled in various ways. The theory of steady planar shock waves is particularly well-developed, and has been studied using the compressible Euler equations, the compressible Navier-Stokes equations, and using kinetic models such as the Boltzmann and Landau equations. For one-dimensional flows, we consider the compressible Euler equation for a polytropic gas,

(1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{x}(\rho u)=0\\
&\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\partial_{x}(\rho u^{2}+\rho T)=0\\
&\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\rho T\right)+\partial_{x}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{3}+\frac{5}{2}\rho uT\right)=0,\end{split}

expressed here in the planar setting for the pressure law
p=\rho T
. Steady shock waves for this equation take the form of jump discontinuities moving at speed
\mathsf{s}
, with constant data
(\rho_{-},u_{-},T_{-})
and
(\rho_{+},u_{+},T_{+})
to the left and right of the discontinuity respecively
[
16
]
. Such shock solutions have been constructed for boundary data and shock speed satisfying the Rankine-Hugoniot and Lax entropy conditions
[
49
]
. Here
(\rho,u,T)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
represent the density, velocity, and temperature, where the velocity
(u,0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is taken parallel to the
x
axis.

When viscous and thermal conduction effects are considered, as in the compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
23
), shock solutions
(\rho,u,T)(t,x)=(\rho,u,T)(x-\mathsf{s}t)
are known to exist with the same speed
\mathsf{s}
and boundary conditions
(\rho_{\pm},u_{\pm},T_{\pm})

[
30
]
. For small-amplitude shocks, the compressible Navier Stokes equations provide solutions close to experimental observations, however, discrepancies are observed inside the shock layer for strong shocks, where more accurate results have been provided by the Boltzmann equation, which models gas dynamics using a statistical description of molecular interactions. Shock profiles for the Boltzmann equation, which travel with speed
\mathsf{s}
, interpolate smoothly between the velocity distributions

(2)

\displaystyle F_{\pm}(\xi)=\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}F(x,\xi)=\frac{\rho_{\pm}}{(2\pi T_{\pm})^{3/2}}e^{-\left|\xi-u_{\pm}e_{1}\right|^{2}/T_{\pm}}

taking limits in
L^{1}(1+\left|\xi\right|^{2})
,
for
(\rho_{\pm},u_{\pm},T_{\pm})
and
\mathsf{s}
satisfying the Rankine-Hugoniot and Lax entropy conditions, where
F_{\pm}(\xi)
represent equilibrium solutions to the Boltzmann equation. Such solutions have been modelled numerically
[
10
]
, providing results close to experiment
[
50
]

[
7
]

[
27
]
. In this work, we construct small-amplitude shock profiles to the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation with hard potentials, corresponding to power-law intermolecular interactions. The non-cutoff Boltzmann equation
is a formal statistical model developed by Maxwell for collisional gases interacting by long-range interaction potentials
[
54
]
, which is considered a more realistic assumption on the microscopic dynamics. More precisely, we construct solutions to the traveling wave non-cutoff Boltzmann equation

(3)

\displaystyle(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}F=Q(F,F),\qquad(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

The collision term
Q
is a bilinear operator defined by

(4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q(G,F)(\xi)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi_{*}}}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\sigma}}\!B\left(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\sigma\cdot\frac{\xi-\xi_{*}}{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|}\right)\left[G(\xi_{*}^{\prime})F(\xi^{\prime})-G(\xi_{*})F(\xi)\right]\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\end{split}

where

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi+\xi_{*}}{2}+\frac{\xi-\xi_{*}}{2}\sigma,\quad\xi_{*}^{\prime}=\frac{\xi+\xi_{*}}{2}+\frac{\xi-\xi_{*}}{2}\sigma,

and where we define the angle

(5)

\displaystyle\cos\theta=\sigma\cdot\frac{\xi-\xi_{*}}{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|},

for some nonnegative collision kernel
B\geq 0
. We remark that when
F=G
, we can symmetrize the collision kernel
B
as

\displaystyle\bar{B}\left(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta\right)=\left[B\left(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta\right)+B\left(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,-\cos\theta\right)\right]1_{\cos\theta\geq 0},

using the integral transformation
\sigma\mapsto-\sigma
and interchanging
\xi^{\prime}
and
\xi_{*}^{\prime}
, so we may assume that
B
is supported on
\theta\in(0,\pi/2]
. We make the following further hypotheses.

The collision kernel.

We impose the following assumptions on the collision kernel
B(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)
.

•

We assume
B(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)
can be decomposed in the form

(6)

\displaystyle B(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)=\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{\gamma}b_{s}(\cos\theta)

for some
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and some measurable function
b_{s}\geq 0
.

•

We assume the angular term has bounds

(7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{c_{b}\theta^{1+2s}}\leq b_{s}(\cos\theta)\sin\theta\leq\frac{c_{b}}{\theta^{1+2s}},\qquad&\theta\in\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right],\\
b_{s}(\cos\theta)=0,\qquad&\theta\in\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi\right],\end{split}

for some
c_{b}>0
and some
s\in(0,1)
.

•

We assume
hard potential
interactions, or more precisely, that

(8)

\displaystyle s\in(0,1/2),\qquad\gamma\in(0,1).

We will assume that the conditions (
6
), (
7
), and (
8
) hold for the collision kernel
B
and the associated collision operator
Q
for the rest of this article.
We note that hypothesis (
7
) ensures that the collision operator (
4
) is a singular integral. The main motivation for these assumptions comes from particles interacting according to the radial power law potential

\displaystyle\phi(r)=\frac{1}{r^{p-1}},\qquad p\in(5,\infty).

The collision kernel for such potentials was first computed in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by Maxwell
[
54
]
in 1867, who showed assumptions (
6
), (
7
), and (
8
) are satisfied for the parameters
s=1/(p-1)
and
\gamma=(p-5)/(p-1)
(see Chapter II, Section 5 of
[
13
]
). Moreover, simulations of Boltzmann shock profiles for the power
p=10
have shown particularly good agreement with experiment for monoatomic gases
[
26
]
, further motivating our work.

We now recall basic facts about the collision operator. We have the identities

(9)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!Q(F,F)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi\\
\frac{1}{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,{\rm d}\xi=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

corresponding to conservation of the number of particles, and conservation of momentum and energy in elastic collisions. If we define the reflection operators

(10)

\displaystyle R_{2}F(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})=F(\xi_{1},-\xi_{2},\xi_{3}),\qquad R_{3}F(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})=F(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},-\xi_{3}),

we note that by the commutator identities
[R_{2},\xi_{1}\partial_{x}]=[R_{3},\xi_{1}\partial_{x}]=0
and invariance of the collision operator
Q
under Galilean transformations,
R_{2}F
and
R_{3}F
will also be solutions to (
3
). By the choice of boundary conditions (
2
), we have
R_{2}F_{\pm}=R_{3}F_{\pm}=F_{\pm}
, and therefore, it is reasonable to assume that any unique solution
F
to (
3
) with these boundary conditions will satisfy

R_{2}F=R_{3}F=F,

so we require this condition on
F
for the rest of this section. The main advantage of this condition will be to simplify the 1D viscous shock stability analysis in Section
6
, however it could be removed at the cost of more intricate fluid stability estimates. If we further assume that
F>0
and
F\in L^{\infty}_{x}L^{1}_{\xi}(1+\left|\xi\right|^{2})
, then we can define the associated macroscopic quantities

(11)

\displaystyle I[F]:=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
m\\
E\end{pmatrix}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!F(\xi)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\frac{1}{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,{\rm d}\xi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}

which are preserved by collisions,
where
m
denotes the momentum, and
E
the energy, which can re-express as

(12)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{0}(\rho,u,T):=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
\rho u\\
\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\rho T\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
m\\
E\end{pmatrix}

and we see that

(13)

(\rho,u,T)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}

holds whenever the integral in (
11
) is well-defined. For any hydrodynamic quantities
(\rho,u,T)
satisfying (
13
), we can define the associated Maxwellian distribution

(14)

\displaystyle M_{(\rho,u,T)}(\xi)=\frac{\rho}{(2\pi T)^{3/2}}e^{-\left|\xi-ue_{1}\right|^{2}/T}

satisfying

(15)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!M_{(\rho,u,T)}(\xi)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\frac{1}{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,{\rm d}\xi=\mathsf{f}_{0}(\rho,u,T),

and which is an equilibrium for the collision operator in the sense that

Q\left(M_{(\rho,u,T)},M_{(\rho,u,T)}\right)=0

holds, and it can be shown from the
H
-theorem that all sufficiently regular and decaying functions
F>0
satisfying
Q(F,F)=0
are of this form (see Chapter 3 of
[
14
]
). For small-amplitude shocks, it is reasonable to assume that solutions will be close to equilibrium, so computing the moment identities

(16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!M_{(\rho,u,T)}(\xi)\xi_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\frac{1}{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,{\rm d}\xi&=\begin{pmatrix}\rho u\\
\rho u^{2}+\rho T\\
\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{3}+\frac{5}{2}\rho uT\end{pmatrix}\\[3.0pt]
&=:\mathsf{f}_{1}(\rho,u,T),\end{split}

we see that solutions to the Boltzmann equation (
3
) can be formally approximated by
M_{(\rho,u,T)}
for solutions
(\rho,u,T)(t,x)=(\rho,u,T)(x-\mathsf{s}t)
to the Euler equation (
1
).
Integrating (
3
) in space with boundary conditions (
2
), we get the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions

(17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{f}_{1}(\rho_{-},u_{-},T_{-})-\mathsf{f}_{1}(\rho_{+},u_{+},T_{+})=\mathsf{s}\big[\mathsf{f}_{0}(\rho_{-},u_{-},T_{-})-\mathsf{f}_{0}(\rho_{+},u_{+},T_{+})\big].\end{split}

For any left reference state
(\rho_{-},u_{-},T_{-})
satisfying (
13
), we define the
Rankine-Hugoniot locus
as the set of
(\rho_{+},u_{+},T_{+})
satisfying (
17
) for some
\mathsf{s}\in\mathbb{R}
. To solve for
(\rho_{+},u_{+},T_{+})
sufficiently close to
(\rho_{-},u_{-},T_{-})
, we rewrite the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions in terms of the conservative variables
(\rho,m,E)
defined in (
12
), giving

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{f}_{1}\circ\mathsf{f}_{0}^{-1}\right)(\rho_{-},m_{-},E_{-})-\left(\mathsf{f}_{1}\circ\mathsf{f}_{0}^{-1}\right)(\rho_{+},m_{+},E_{+})=\mathsf{s}\left[(\rho_{-},m_{-},E_{-})-(\rho_{+},m_{+},E_{+})\right],

which we see is an approximate eigenvalue problem for
\left(\mathsf{f}_{1}\circ\mathsf{f}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}(\rho_{-},m_{-},E_{-})
. If we compute the derivatives

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime}(\rho,u,T)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
u&\rho&0\\
\frac{1}{2}u^{2}+\frac{3}{2}T&\rho u&\frac{3}{2}\rho\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{1}^{\prime}(\rho,u,T)=\begin{pmatrix}u&\rho&0\\
u^{2}+T&2\rho u&\rho\\
\frac{1}{2}\left|u\right|^{2}u+\frac{5}{2}uT&\frac{3}{2}\rho u^{2}+\frac{5}{2}\rho T&\frac{5}{2}\rho u\end{pmatrix},

we can then compute that the matrix
\mathsf{f}_{1}^{\prime}(\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime})^{-1}(\rho,m,E)
has the distinct eigenvalues

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=u-\mathsf{c},\quad\lambda_{2}=u,\quad\lambda_{3}=u+\mathsf{c}

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}=\sqrt{\frac{5T}{3}}

is the speed of sound. By standard perturbation theory for simple eigenvalues, this implies that the shock speed
\mathsf{s}
will be close to
\lambda_{i}
for some
i=1,2,3
. It will be useful to compute the right eigenvectors in
(\rho,u,T)
coordinates, namely we have vectors

(18)

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{1}=\frac{1}{4\mathsf{c}}\begin{pmatrix}-3\rho\\
3\mathsf{c}\\
-2T\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{r}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-3\rho\\
0\\
u^{2}+3T\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{r}_{3}=\frac{1}{4\mathsf{c}}\begin{pmatrix}3\rho\\
3\mathsf{c}\\
2T\end{pmatrix},

which satisfy the generalized eigenvalue problem

(19)

\displaystyle\left[\lambda_{i}\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}_{1}^{\prime}\right]\mathbf{r}_{i}=0,\qquad i=1,2,3.

We can now characterize the Rankine-Hugoniot locus more precisely. Writing
V=(\rho,u,T)
for the hydrodynamic variables, in a neighbourhood of
V_{-}
, by an implicit function theory argument, we see that the Rankine-Hugoniot locus then consists of three smooth curves intersecting at
V_{-}
and tangent to
\mathbf{r}_{i}(V_{-})
at
V_{-}
, for
i=1,2,3
(cf. Chapter 8 of
[
20
]
). To construct shocks in the kinetic or fluid setting, we must also impose the
Lax entropy condition

(20)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(V_{+})\leq\mathsf{s}\leq\lambda_{i}(V_{-}),

first introduced in
[
48
]
, which guarantees that the end states
(V_{-},V_{+,i}(\epsilon))
correspond to admissible jump solutions to the Euler equations (
1
) travelling with speed
\mathsf{s}_{i}(\epsilon)
. The eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}
are genuinely nonlinear, satisfying
\mathbf{r}_{1}(V)\cdot\nabla\lambda_{1}(V)=\mathbf{r}_{3}(V)\cdot\nabla\lambda_{3}(V)=1
by our normalization, and correspond to leftward or rightward compressive shocks, while the middle eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
is linearly degenerate in the sense that
\mathbf{r}_{2}(V)\cdot\nabla\lambda_{2}(V)=0
, and corresponds to a contact discontinuity. For the genuinely nonlinear eigenvalues
i=1,3
, the Rankine-Hugoniot curve
V_{+,i}(\epsilon)
can be parametrized such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\big(V_{+,i}(\epsilon)\big)=\lambda_{i}(V_{-})-\epsilon

with asymptotics

(21)

\displaystyle V_{+,i}(\epsilon)=V_{-}-\epsilon\mathbf{r}_{i}(V_{-})+O(\epsilon^{2})

and shock speed

(22)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{i}(\epsilon)=\lambda_{i}(V_{-})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}+O(\epsilon^{2})

(see Theorem 5.14 of
[
39
]
), such that the Lax entropy condition (
20
) is satisfied for
\epsilon>0
. For kinetic shocks with small amplitude
\epsilon
, we make the assumption of a length scale
1/\epsilon
, so that the Knuden number, measuring the ratio of the mean free path to the length scale, is of order
\epsilon
. In this scaling, we can formally derive the compressible Navier-Stokes equations

(23)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
\rho u\\
\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\rho T\end{pmatrix}+\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\rho u\\
\rho u^{2}+\rho T\\
\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{3}+\frac{5}{2}\rho uT\end{pmatrix}=\partial_{x}\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&\mu&0\\
0&\mu u&\varkappa\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
u\\
T\end{pmatrix}\right]

as a second order Chapman-Enskog expansion for the Boltzmann equation (cf.
[
44
]

[
8
]
),
where the viscosity and thermal conductivity
\mu,\varkappa
are defined by

\displaystyle\mu=-T^{1-\gamma/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\left(\xi_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\right)\underline{L}^{-1}\left[\left(\xi_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\right)\underline{M}\right]\,{\rm d}\xi>0,

\displaystyle\varkappa=-T^{1-\gamma/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\xi_{1}\left(\left|\xi\right|^{2}-5\right)\underline{L}^{-1}\left[\xi_{1}\left(\left|\xi\right|^{2}-5\right)\underline{M}\right]\,{\rm d}\xi>0,

where we have defined the reference Maxwellian

\displaystyle\underline{M}(\xi)=M_{(1,0,1)}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{-\left|\xi\right|^{2}/2},

and the reference linearized collision operator

\displaystyle\underline{L}f=Q(\underline{M},f)+Q(f,\underline{M}).

We fix the left reference state
V_{-}=(\rho_{-},u_{-},T_{-})=(1,0,1)
, and let
V_{+}=V_{+,3}(\epsilon)
be the associated right hand state on the third Rankine-Hugoniot curve, uniquely determined such that
\lambda_{3}(V_{+})=\lambda_{3}(V_{-})-\epsilon
for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, and we let
\mathsf{s}=\mathsf{s}_{3}(\epsilon)
be the associated shock speed. For such boundary data, we let
(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{T})(t,x)=(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{T})(x-\mathsf{s}t)
be a smooth solution to (
23
), which we know exists uniquely up to translation
[
30
]
.

We now define the spaces in which we will construct solutions. We first define Hilbert space norm

\displaystyle\left\|F\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}=\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}F\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

and the associated Hilbert space

(24)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{0}=\left\{F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\,\middle|\,\left\|F\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}<\infty,R_{2}F=R_{3}F=F\right\}.

A perturbative global-well posedness theory for solutions
F=\underline{M}+\underline{M}^{1/2}f
to the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation was developed by the group of Alexandre, Morimoto, Ukai, Xu, and Yang in the papers
[
5
]

[
4
]

[
6
]
,
and simultaneously by Gressman and Strain
[
33
]
. For our result, we work in the norm defined in
[
33
]
,

(25)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}=\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma+2s}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\left(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle\left\langle\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle\right)^{\frac{\gamma+2s+1}{2}}\frac{(f^{\prime}-f)^{2}}{d(\xi,\xi^{\prime})^{3+2s}}1_{d(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq 1}\,{\rm d}\xi^{\prime}\,{\rm d}\xi,

for the bracket
\left\langle\xi\right\rangle=(1+\left|\xi\right|^{2})^{1/2}
, where the metric

\displaystyle d(\xi,\xi^{\prime})=\sqrt{\left|\xi-\xi^{\prime}\right|^{2}+\left(\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\left|\xi^{\prime}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}/4}

is the distance on a lifted paraboloid. We additionally have the norm bounds

(26)

\displaystyle\left\|\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2+s}f\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}f\right\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}\lesssim\left\|\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2+s}f\right\|_{H^{s}}

proved in Section 2 of
[
33
]
, where the Sobolev norms
H^{s}
are defined by
\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}_{\xi}}={\|\!\left\langle\zeta\right\rangle^{s}\!\hat{f}(\zeta)\|}_{L^{2}_{\zeta}}
where
\hat{f}(\zeta)
represents the Fourier transform of
f
. The well-posedness theory in
[
33
]
depends on precise coercivity estimates for the linearized operator
\underline{L}
and trilinear estimates for the nonlinearity, proved by an intricate Littlewood-Paley argument on the lifted paraboloid, and we make use of these estimates extensively in our result. More precisely, we define the norm

(27)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}=\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}},

and we define the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
as the set of measurable
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\in N^{s,\gamma}
and
R_{2}f=R_{3}f=f
. We see from (
26
) and the hard potential assumption (
8
) that we have the norm bound
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}
for some
C>0
, implying the norm bound

(28)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}.

Finally, for any separable Banach space
\mathsf{E}
and any integer
k\geq 0
, we define the rescaled Bochner-Sobolev space norms

\displaystyle\left\|g\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathsf{E}}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\epsilon^{1-2k}\left\|\partial_{x}^{j}g\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R};\mathsf{E})}^{2},

and similarly define the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathsf{E}}=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{H^{0}_{\epsilon}\mathsf{E}}
.

We now present the main result of this article.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
for
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(k)>0
sufficiently small, let assumptions (
6
), (
7
), and (
8
) hold on the collision kernel, and let
(\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{T}_{\mathrm{NS}})(x-\mathsf{s}t)
be a shock solution to the compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
23
) with boundary conditions

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to-\infty}\left(\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{T}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right)(x)=V_{-}:=(1,0,1),

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to+\infty}\left(\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{T}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right)(x)=V_{+,3}(\epsilon),

and shock speed
\mathsf{s}=\mathsf{s}_{3}(\epsilon)
. Write
M_{\mathrm{NS}}=M_{(\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}},\bar{T}_{\mathrm{NS}})}
for the lifted Navier-Stokes shock profile.
Then for any
k\geq 2
, there exists a solution
F(x,\xi)
to the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation

\displaystyle(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}F=Q(F,F),\qquad(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3},

satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|F-M_{\mathrm{NS}}\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq C_{k}\epsilon^{2}.

Furthermore,
F
is unique up to translation among solutions
\bar{F}
satisfying the symmetries
R_{2}\bar{F}=R_{3}\bar{F}=\bar{F}
and the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{F}-M_{\mathrm{NS}}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq c_{1}\epsilon

for
c_{1}>0
sufficiently small.

In a future work, we expect to remove the symmetry condition
R_{2}\bar{F}=R_{3}\bar{F}=\bar{F}
in the uniqueness class of the data. We note crucially that we are not able to prove positivity of the resulting shock profiles
F\geq 0
. Currently, positivity of kinetic shock profiles is only known to hold for the Boltzmann equation with hard sphere interactions
[
52
]
. In a future work, we plan to prove stronger regularity and sharper decay estimates the velocity variable
\xi
, as well as stretched exponential decay estimates in
x
, for the shock profile
F
.

We compare this with other works in the literature. The theory of inviscid fluid shock waves in one dimension is very old, and was developed by Riemann
[
61
]
, though under an incorrect assumption of isentropic flow, as noted by Rayleigh, who proved entropy dissipation across shock fronts by vanishing viscosity arguments
[
60
]
. One-dimensional shock waves for general viscous gas dynamics were first constructed by Gilbarg
[
30
]
, and general solutions to the inviscid Riemann problem were developed by Lax
[
48
]
. The theory of one-dimensional kinetic shocks is somewhat more recent. Kinetic shock profiles have been rigorously constructed by Nicolaenko and Thurber for the Boltzmann equation with hard sphere interactions
[
59
]
, a result which was generalized by Caflisch and Nicolaenko for hard cutoff particle interactions
[
11
]
, proving unique solutions up to translation, based on bifurcation arguments and Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction, with uniform bounds

\displaystyle\left|F(x,\xi)-M_{\mathrm{NS}}(x,\xi)\right|\leq C_{r}\epsilon^{2}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-r}\underline{M}^{1/2}\left(e^{-\mu\left|x\right|^{\beta}-\tau\epsilon\left|x\right|}\right)

and

\displaystyle\left|F(x,\xi)-M_{\mathrm{NS}}(\xi)\right|\leq C_{\alpha,r}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-r}\underline{M}^{1/2+\alpha}

for any
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
and
r\geq 0
, for some constants
\mu,\tau>0
and
\beta\in(0,1)
corresponding to stretched exponential spatial decay. Positivity of solutions for hard sphere interactions was proved by the stability theory of Liu and Yu
[
52
]
using an orthogonal micro-macro decomposition and novel energy estimates developed in
[
51
]
. Adapting these methods, Métivier and Zumbrun constructed positive shock profile solutions to the hard sphere problem
[
56
]
with sharper estimates, reducing (
3
) to a problem with bounded operators by the normalization
\tilde{A}=\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-1}\xi_{1}
and
\tilde{Q}=\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-1/2}Q(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-1/2}\cdot,\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-1/2}\cdot)
. This result was generalized by Albritton, Bedrossian, and Novack for the considerably more singular Landau equation with Coulomb potentials in the plasma setting
[
1
]
, proving bounds

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\delta\left\langle\epsilon x\right\rangle^{1/2}}\underline{M}^{-q}\epsilon^{-\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\left(F-M_{\mathrm{NS}}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,\xi}}\leq C_{N,q}\epsilon^{2}

for
\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq N
and
q\in[0,1)
, for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small with respect to
N,q
, for some constants
C,\delta>0
. We note that these solutions are not known to be positive. To control moment loss, they track multiple velocity norms to prove a priori estimates on a linearized problem, by combining hypocoercive collisional estimates with stability results for viscous shocks. To close a Galerkin scheme, they replace the Landau collision operator
Q_{L}
with a lifted operator
Q_{\kappa}
that gains moments, and which is well-behaved with respect to a Hermite discretization. This introduces a smallness condition on the shock strength
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)
dependent on the lift parameter
\kappa
, which they overcome with careful functional-analytic continuity arguments. Our strategy follows a similar Galerkin scheme, however we obtain simpler a priori estimates, using the fact that the linearized Boltzmann collision operator exhibits a spectral gap for hard potentials, so we only need to track a single velocity norm.

We note parallel works on one-dimensional kinetic shocks for the Bhatnagar-Gross-Krook (BGK) equation, particularly by the group of Cuesta, Hittmeir, and Schmeiser, who proved existence and stability of BGK shocks for scalar conservation laws
[
19
]
and later for isentropic gases
[
18
]
, exploiting a micro-macro decomposition in the spirit of
[
52
]
and stability results for viscous shocks
[
43
]

[
31
]
. A general framework for existence and stability of BGK shocks was developed in
[
17
]
, whose presentation influences this work. The problem of kinetic shocks in three dimensions is much more challenging and will not be addressed in this work, so we refer to the recent 3D stability result
[
21
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

The basic strategy of our proof follows the methods of Métivier and Zumbrun
[
56
]
and Albritton, Bedrossian, and Novack
[
1
]
, namely, we use the Chapman-Enskog expansion to approximate solutions to the problem (
3
) by shock solutions to the 1D compressible Navier-Stokes equations, we linearize around the Chapman-Enskog approximation, and then close a fixed point argument. To show existence of solutions, we use a Galerkin scheme and reduce to solving a linear ODE problem, where we construct solutions in
L^{2}_{x}
by stable/unstable dimensionality arguments.

One of the key tools we will use is the micro-macro decomposition, first developed by Hilbert in the theory of hydrodynamic limits
[
38
]
, and applied to kinetic shock profiles by
[
52
]
. By classical properties of linearized collisions (cf. Theorem 7.2.1 of
[
14
]
), we know that the linearized operator
\underline{L}
is negative semi-definite

\displaystyle\left\langle\underline{L}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq 0

in the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
, and has a three dimensional kernel

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}:=\ker\left(\underline{L}|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\right)

with orthonormal basis

(29)

\displaystyle\left\{\psi_{0},\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\right\}=\left\{\underline{M},\ \xi_{1}\underline{M},\ \frac{\left|\xi\right|^{2}-3}{\sqrt{6}}\underline{M}\right\}.

We note that without the symmetry assumption (
24
) in the definition of
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
, we would have a five-dimensional kernel for the linearized operator
\underline{L}
. We also define the orthogonal projection operator in
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
onto
\mathbb{U}
as

(30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}F

\displaystyle=\underline{M}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\frac{\left|\xi\right|^{2}-3}{\sqrt{6}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!F(\xi)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\frac{\left|\xi\right|^{2}-3}{\sqrt{6}}\end{pmatrix}\,{\rm d}\xi=\underline{M}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\frac{\left|\xi\right|^{2}-3}{\sqrt{6}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
m\\
\frac{2E-3\rho}{\sqrt{6}}\end{pmatrix}.

We see that
\mathbb{P}
is well-defined for any
F\in L^{1}(1+\left|\xi\right|^{2})
, and that by the linear transformation

(31)

\displaystyle\left(I|_{\mathbb{U}}\right)^{-1}:\mathbb{R}_{\rho}\times\mathbb{R}_{m}\times\mathbb{R}_{E}\to\mathbb{U},

we can parametrize the macroscopic space

\mathbb{U}
by the density, momentum and energy coordinates
W=(\rho,m,E)
, which we call the
conservative variables
. Similarly, the smooth, nonlinear transformation

(32)

\left(I|_{\mathbb{U}}\right)^{-1}\circ\mathsf{f}_{0}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{U}

allows us to parametrize the macroscopic space
\mathbb{U}
near the reference state

{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}:=\underline{M}\in\mathbb{U}

by the hydrodynamic variables
V=(\rho,u,T)
, which will simplify the analysis for the rest of the paper.

For the rest of this article, we use

three
different coordinate systems for the macroscopic state. We will use the symbol
V=(\rho,u,T)
for the
hydrodynamic variables
, we will write
W=(\rho,m,E)
for the
conservative variables
, and we use the fraktur symbol
\mathfrak{u}
to denote elements of the tangent space
\mathbb{U}=T_{\underline{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
of the Maxwellian manifold. Elements of
\mathbb{U}
correspond to small fluctuations in the statistical state described by the Boltzmann distribution for large Knudsen numbers, which force data to be approximately locally Maxwellian.

Following the micro-macro decomposition introduced for the Boltzmann equation by Liu and Yu
[
52
]
, we define the orthogonal complement

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}:=\mathbb{U}^{\perp}=\ker\left(\mathbb{P}|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\right)\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{0}

which we consider as the space of
microscopic quantities
,
giving the orthogonal decomposition

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{0}=\mathbb{U}\oplus\mathbb{V}.

Any function
f
such that
\mathbb{P}f=0
will be called
purely microscopic
. By (
9
), the collision term
Q(F,F)
is purely microscopic, as are the linearized operators
f\mapsto Q(f,g)+Q(g,f)
for any
g
. The terminology is explained as follows: any perturbation
f\in\mathbb{V}
cannot be directly observed from the hydrodynamic quantities of density, momentum, or energy, and can therefore be considered as a microscopic kinetic refinement to the macroscopic fluid model.

We note that
\mathbb{U}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
by the Sobolev norm bounds in (
26
), and therefore we can similarly decompose

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{1}=\mathbb{U}+\mathbb{V}^{1},\qquad\mathbb{U}\cap\mathbb{V}^{1}=\left\{0\right\}

where we define
\mathbb{V}^{1}={\mathcal{H}}^{1}\cap\mathbb{V}
.
A similar micro-macro decomposition exists for dual spaces. Using the inclusion of spaces
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\subset N^{s,\gamma}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
where
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
denotes the space of Schwartz functions, we have the inclusion of dual spaces
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\subset(N^{s,\gamma})^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and therefore we may consider
(N^{s,\gamma})^{\prime}
as a Hilbert space contained in the set of tempered distributions. Writing
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})=C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\prime}
for the space of distributions (cf.
[
32
]
),
we define the space

(33)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}=\left\{g\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\ \middle|\ \underline{M}^{-1/2}g\in\left(N^{s,\gamma}\right)^{\prime},\ R_{2}g=R_{3}g=g\right\}

with associated norm

\displaystyle\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}=\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\right\|_{(N^{s,\gamma})^{\prime}},

where we note that the reflection operators
R_{2}
and
R_{3}
on
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
can be continuously extended to
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and we have the inclusion of spaces
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\subset\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, so the conditions in (
33
) are well-defined. Furthermore, since
g\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
implies
\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the quantity

(34)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\left|\xi\right|^{2}\end{pmatrix}g(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi=\left\langle\underline{M}^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\xi_{1}\\
\left|\xi\right|^{2}\end{pmatrix},\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\right\rangle_{\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\,\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

is a well-defined continuous operator in
g\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
, and therefore the hydrodynamic projection operator
\mathbb{P}
may be extended continuously to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}:{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\to\mathbb{U},

with kernel

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}^{-1}=\ker\left(\mathbb{P}|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}\right)

corresponding to the space of purely microscopic functions in
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
.

2.1.
Maxwellian equilibrium manifold

It will be useful to characterize the set of Maxwellian equilibria more precisely. If we define the Maxwellian manifold

(35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}=\left\{M_{(\rho,u,T)}\,\Big|\,\left|(\rho,u,T)-(1,0,1)\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}\right\}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{0}

for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, we see by direct computation that the macroscopic space
\mathbb{U}
is simply the tangent space of
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}
at
\underline{M}
. Using the identity

\displaystyle I[M_{(\rho,u,T)}]=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
\rho u\\
\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\rho T\end{pmatrix}

by (
11
) (
15
) and the definition of the macroscopic projection (
30
), we see that for
\epsilon_{0}
sufficiently small, we can parametrize the Maxwellians
M_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}
by macroscopic states
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathbb{U}
such that
\left|\mathfrak{u}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\lesssim\epsilon_{0}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}M_{\mathfrak{u}}=\mathfrak{u}.

This micro-macro formalism, in which fluid states are parametrized directly by the macroscopic Hilbert space
\mathbb{U}
was used in
[
56
]

[
57
]
, see also
[
12
]
for an analogous construction in incompressible hydrodynamic limits.

We now investigate differentiability properties of the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}
. For all integer
\ell\geq 0
, we define the weighted Sobolev norm

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{H^{\ell}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq\ell}\left\|\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2},

and we define the conjugated Hilbert space
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
as the set of all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
such that
\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\in H^{\ell}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
in the sense of weak derivatives for all multi-indices
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}
satisfying
\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq\ell
, with Hilbert space norm

(36)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}=\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{H^{\ell}_{\ell}}^{2}.

We then have the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small. Then the Maxwellian manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}
is a smooth submanifold of
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
for all
\ell\geq 0
, and in particular,
M_{\epsilon_{0}}
is a smooth submanifold of
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
.
Furthermore, for any
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}\leq C_{\ell}\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|

for all
\ell\geq 0
.

Proof.

The proof of the first statement is a straightforward computation, using the definitions of the spaces
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
and
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
and bounds on the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
. Using the hydrodynamic variables
V=(\rho,u,T)
for
\mathbb{U}
, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
we can compute the derivative

(37)

\displaystyle\partial_{V}^{\alpha}M_{V}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\rho}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{u}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{T}^{\alpha_{3}}\left(\frac{\rho}{(2\pi T)^{3/2}}e^{-\left|\xi-ue_{1}\right|^{2}/2T}\right)

(38)

\displaystyle=p_{\alpha}(\rho,u,T^{-1/2},\xi)e^{-\left|\xi-ue_{1}\right|^{2}/2T}

where
p_{\alpha}(\rho,u,T^{-1/2},\xi)
is a polynomial in all its variables. To show that this lies in
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\underline{M}^{-1/2}\partial_{V}^{\alpha}M_{V}(\xi)=cp_{\alpha}(\rho,u,T^{-1/2},\xi)e^{-\left|\xi-ue_{1}\right|^{2}/2T+\left|\xi\right|^{2}/4}

is Schwartz class in
\xi
, but this clearly holds for any temperature
T<2
, and therefore for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small. To prove the last claim, we note by (
26
) and the hard cutoff assumption (
8
) that we have the continuous inclusion of spaces
\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
.

To prove the microscopic estimate, we note that since
\,{\rm d}M_{{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}}\in\mathbb{U}
by definition of
\mathbb{U}
, so that
(I-\mathbb{P})\,{\rm d}M_{{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}}=0
. Boundedness of the operator
\mathbb{P}:\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}\to\mathbb{U}
and smoothness of
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}
then proves the claim for sufficiently small
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
∎

Since we are constructing our shock profile around the travelling wave Maxwellian
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
and not the steady state
\underline{M}
, it will be useful to define the uncentred linearized operator

\displaystyle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}f=Q(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},f)+Q(f,M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})

for
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}
. Differentiating the identity

\displaystyle 0=\,{\rm d}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\big[Q(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})\big]=Q(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})+Q(\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},

we can define the uncentred projection operator

\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}=\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathbb{P}.

To see that this operator is a projection, we differentiate
\mathbb{P}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}=\bar{\mathfrak{u}}
to get the identity

(39)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}=I,

giving that
\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}^{2}=\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}
.

2.2.
Strategy of the proof

We can now outline the main methods of the proof of Theorem
1.1
. We start with a travelling wave solution
\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x-\mathsf{s}t)
solution to the
1D Navier-Stokes equations (
23
), with boundary conditions
V_{\pm}
defined in Theorem
1.1
, and we let
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}=(I|_{\mathbb{U}})^{-1}\circ\mathsf{f}_{0}(\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS}})
be the
\mathbb{U}
-valued change of coordinates using the formula in (
32
). In Section
3
, we define a perturbative problem

(40)

\displaystyle F=M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}+f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]+f

from the Chapman Enskog expansion, where the second order term
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
is a microscopic correction of order
O(\epsilon^{2})
defined in (
57
),
and we then reformulate (
3
) as a linearized problem

(41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f:=(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f=\mathcal{E}[f]

for a microscopic error term
\mathcal{E}[f]\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}
defined in (
66
). We will find that
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
has a one-dimensional kernel due to translation symmetry of the problem, which we fix using the following proposition.

Proposition 2.2
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
admits a right inverse

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{\dagger}:H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}\to H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}

and a unit vector
\ell_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{U}
depending continuously on
\epsilon
, such that for any
z\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}
, the function
f=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{\dagger}z
solves the problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f=z,\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=0.

This operator satisfies the estimate

(42)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{\dagger}z\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}},

and furthermore, we have the bound
\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(0)\right|\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{2}
, for constants
C_{k},c_{0}>0
independent of
\epsilon
.

The last bound is a nondegeneracy condition, which will allow us to prove uniqueness of solutions up to translation. The proof of this proposition occupies most of the article, and combined with bounds on
\mathcal{E}[f]
and
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
, this will prove the main theorem by a fixed point argument. To prove this proposition, we follow the strategy of
[
56
]
. In Section
5
, we prove a priori estimates on the linearized problem, exploiting the compensator theory developed by Kawashima
[
42
]
, and close an energy estimate up to a small macroscopic error
\epsilon\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}
. To bound this error, we use a stability result for viscous shocks, which allows us to close estimates on the linearized operator in Section
6
. The explicit construction of the right inverse then proceeds by a Galerkin scheme. For non-cutoff Boltzmann, however, additional difficulties arise due to the loss of velocity moments in the Galerkin scheme, as identified by Albritton, Bedrossian, and Novack
for the Coulombic Landau equation
[
1
]
, and therefore we will require a lifting procedure on the collision operator
Q
, and functional-analaytic continuity methods to show that this procedure can be justified. We adapt the lifting method of
[
1
]
to the Boltzmann collision operator.

2.3.
Estimates on the collision operator

We now review basic boundedness and coercivity estimates for the non-cutoff Boltzmann collision operator, following the work of
[
33
]
. We use the following results.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any
f,g,h\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
as defined in (
27
), we have the estimates

(43)

\displaystyle\left\langle Q(g,h),f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}

and

(44)

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta_{0}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}

for some constants
\delta_{0},C>0
.

Proof.

Using Theorem 2.1 of
[
33
]
and the hard potential assumption (
8
), we have the trilinear estimate

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle Q(g,h),f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left\langle\underline{M}^{-1/2}Q(g,h),\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}h\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}

\displaystyle=C\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\left\|h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}},

where in the last line we have used the norm bound (
28
).
Similarly, using Theorem 8.1 of
[
33
]
, we get the coercivity estimate

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\underline{M}^{-1/2}\underline{L}f,\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{0}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}^{2}=\delta_{0}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2},

by the definition of the norms
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
in (
24
) and (
27
), proving (
44
).
∎

It will turn out to be useful to restate the bound (
43
) in terms of dual spaces, giving the following corollary.

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
g,h\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
, then we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|Q(g,h)\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}\leq C\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}

for some constant
C>0
.

Proof.

The estimate is immediate from the definition (
33
) of the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
.
∎

For
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}
close to
{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}
, we have the following approximate coercivity estimate, analogous to Lemma
2.3
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
, and suppose
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\lesssim\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small. Then we have the estimate

\displaystyle-\left\langle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}-C\epsilon\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}

Proof.

We first re-express

\displaystyle-\left\langle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\underline{L}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\left\langle Q(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M},f)+Q(f,M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M}),f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\underline{L}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\left\langle Q(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M},f)+Q(f,M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M}),(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

where in the last identity we have used that the correction term
Q(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M},f)+Q(f,M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M})
is purely microscopic by Corollary
2.4
, and by Lemma
2.3
, this can then be bounded from below by

\displaystyle\delta_{0}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}-C\left\|M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq-\delta_{0}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}-C^{\prime}\epsilon\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}-C^{\prime}\epsilon\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}

using the bound
\left\|M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-\underline{M}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq C\epsilon
from Lemma
2.1
, and where the last bound holds for
\epsilon
sufficiently small.
∎

We now wish to introduce a ”lifted” collision operator
Q_{\kappa}
for
\kappa>0
, such that
Q_{\kappa}
exhibits improved moment estimates, which will be useful to show existence from a Galerkin scheme. We first write
B_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)
for a collision kernel satisfying hypotheses (
6
) and (
7
) but not necessarily (
8
), with coefficients
s^{\prime}\in(0,1)
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Setting

\displaystyle Q_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(G,F)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi_{*}}}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\sigma}}\!B_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}\left(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\sigma\cdot\frac{\xi-\xi_{*}}{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|}\right)\left[G(\xi_{*}^{\prime})F(\xi^{\prime})-G(\xi_{*})F(\xi)\right]\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*},

we can then define the lifted operator
Q_{\kappa}
as

(45)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{\kappa}(G,F)&=Q(G,F)+\kappa Q_{s,2-2s}(G,F)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi_{*}}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\sigma}}\!\left(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{\gamma}+\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{2-2s}\right)b_{s}(\cos\theta)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\left[G(\xi_{*}^{\prime})F(\xi^{\prime})-G(\xi_{*})F(\xi)\right]\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\end{split}

for any
\kappa\geq 0
. We analogously define the linearized collision operator
\underline{L}_{\kappa}=\underline{L}+\kappa\underline{L}_{s,2-2s}
, where we set

\displaystyle\underline{L}_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}f=Q_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(\underline{M},f)+Q_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(f,\underline{M}).

We then define the associated norm

(46)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}=\max\left\{\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}},\kappa^{1/2}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{{N}^{s,2-2s}}\right\},

and define
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
as the space of
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
such that this norm is finite.
We see from by the definition (
25
) that the norms
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}
are increasing in
\gamma
, and therefore for all
\kappa>0
, we have the equivalence of norms

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\kappa}}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C_{\kappa}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}

for some constants
C_{\kappa}>0
so that the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
is Banach isomorphic to a Hilbert space. As with the definition of the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
in (
33
), we can define the associated dual space

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}=\left\{g\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\,\middle|\,\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\in(N^{s,2-2s})^{\prime},\ R_{2}g=R_{3}g=g\right\},

using the continuous inclusion of spaces
(N^{s,\gamma})^{\prime}\subset(N^{s,2-2s})^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which guarantees that the norm

(47)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\\
&\quad=\inf\left\{\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}g_{0}\right\|_{(N^{s,\gamma})^{\prime}}+\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}g_{1}\right\|_{(N^{s,2-2s})^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,g=g_{0}+g_{1}\right\},\end{split}

is well-defined. By standard identities in interpolation theory, for all
\kappa>0
, we have the bounds

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle g,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\right|\leq\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

for all
g\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
and
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, which are sharp in the sense that

\displaystyle\sup_{\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}\leq 1}}\left|\left\langle g,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\right|=\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

holds for all
g\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}

(cf. Exercise 3.3 of
[
9
]
). Since
g\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
implies
\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the same previous identity (
34
) shows that
\mathbb{P}
can be extended continuously to
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
, so we define the kernel

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}=\ker\left(\mathbb{P}|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\right).

We can analogously define the microscopic functions of positive order
\mathbb{V}^{1}_{\kappa}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, and by the inclusion of spaces
\mathbb{U}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
, we get the micro-macro decompositions

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\kappa}^{1}=\mathbb{U}+\mathbb{V}_{\kappa}^{1},\qquad\mathbb{U}\cap\mathbb{V}_{\kappa}^{1}=\left\{0\right\}

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\kappa}^{-1}=\mathbb{U}+\mathbb{V}_{\kappa}^{-1},\qquad\mathbb{U}\cap\mathbb{V}_{\kappa}^{-1}=\left\{0\right\}

for all
\kappa\geq 0
.

We compare the
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
norm to our previously defined norms by the following bound, which will be useful for controlling the transport term in the Galerkin scheme.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for a fixed
\kappa_{0}>0
. Then for all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{1/2}}{C}\left\|\left\langle\xi\right\rangle f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}

for
C
independent of
\kappa
(but dependent on
\kappa_{0}
). Furthermore, we have the uniform coercivity estimate
\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}
for all
\kappa\geq 0
.

Proof.

From (
26
), we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\geq\kappa^{1/2}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}\geq\frac{\kappa^{1/2}}{C}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{1}}=\frac{\kappa^{1/2}}{C}\left\|\left\langle\xi\right\rangle f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}},

proving the lower bound. As in the proof of Lemma
2.1
, the upper bound in (
26
) gives us that

\displaystyle\max\left\{\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}},\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}\right\}\leq C\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{H^{s}_{1}}\leq C^{\prime}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}},

which proves the desired upper bound. The uniform coercivity estimate follows immediately by definition of
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
.
∎

Following
[
33
]
, for any
(s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
and for all
\ell\geq 0
we define the weighted derivative space
N^{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}_{\ell,\ell}
by the norm

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{N^{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}_{\ell,\ell}}^{2}=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq\ell}\left\|\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}f\right\|_{N^{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}}^{2}.

In fact, the norm in
[
33
]
is written in a slightly different form, however this is easily seen to be equivalent by a fractional integration by parts argument. We can define the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}
as the set of all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
such that
\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\in N^{2,2-2s}_{\ell,\ell}
, with norm

(48)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}=\max\left\{\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}},\kappa^{1/2}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}_{\ell,\ell}}\right\}

and define the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}={\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{0}
with the corresponding norm. We then have the following trilinear and coercivity estimates for
Q_{\kappa}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for some fixed
\kappa_{0}
.
Then for all
\ell\geq 0
and
f,g,h\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}
, we have the trilinear estimate

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle Q_{\kappa}(g,h),f\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}\right|\leq C_{\ell}\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}\left\|h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}

for some
C_{\ell}>0
. independent of
\epsilon
and
\kappa
.
For
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, we have the coercivity estimate.

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}_{\kappa}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2},

for some
\delta>0
. Meanwhile, for purely microscopic
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}
,
we can define an equivalent Hilbert norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}}
for
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
independent of
\kappa
, such that we have the estimate

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}_{\kappa}f,f\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}}\geq\delta_{\ell}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}^{2},

that is, the operator
\underline{L}_{\kappa}
is
hypocoercive
on
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
in the sense of
[
34
]
.

The constants
C_{\ell},\delta,\delta_{\ell}
here are all independent of
\kappa
.

Proof.

We first define the nonlinear operator

\Gamma_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(G,H)=\underline{M}^{-1/2}Q_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(\underline{M}^{1/2}G,\underline{M}^{1/2}H)

for
(s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
. Using Lemma 2.2 of
[
33
]
and (
36
), we have that

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle Q_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(g,h),f\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq\ell}\left|\left\langle\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}\Gamma_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}\left(\underline{M}^{-1/2}g,\underline{M}^{-1/2}h\right),\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}(\underline{M}^{-1/2}f)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|

(49)

\displaystyle\leq C_{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\ell}\left\|g\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\right\|_{N^{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}_{\ell,\ell}}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}g\right\|_{N^{s^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}_{\ell,\ell}}

and therefore we have that

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle Q_{\kappa}(g,h),f\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\ell}\left\|g\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}\Big(\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}h\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\kappa\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}h\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}_{\ell,\ell}}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}_{\ell,\ell}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\ell}\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}\left\|h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}},

using the definition of the
{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,\ell}_{\kappa}}
norm in (
48
), proving the first estimate.

For the coercivity estimate, using Theorem 8.1 of
[
33
]
, we have that

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}_{s,2-2s}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta_{0}\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}^{2}

for some
\delta_{0}>0

and therefore we have the estimate

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}_{\kappa}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\underline{L}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}-\kappa\left\langle\underline{L}_{s,2-2s}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

\displaystyle\geq\delta\left(\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{N^{s,\gamma}}^{2}+\kappa\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\delta\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

for some
\delta>0
. The hypocoercivity estimate is then proved similarly, using Lemma 2.6 of
[
33
]
.

∎

As previously, for
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}\in\mathbb{U}
close to
{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}
, we define the uncentred linearized operator

\displaystyle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}f=Q_{\kappa}(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},f)+Q_{\kappa}(f,M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}),

satisfying the identities

(50)

\displaystyle(I-\mathbb{P})L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},

and we have the following approximate coercivity estimate.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small, and let
\kappa_{0}>0
be fixed. Then for any
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
and
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, and any
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}\in\mathbb{U}
such that
\epsilon=\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\langle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}-C\epsilon\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

for constants
\delta,C>0
independent of
\kappa
and
\epsilon
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Lemma
2.5
, using the uniform estimates in
\kappa
from Lemma
2.7
, the fact that the Maxwellian manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}
embeds smoothly in
\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
, and the uniform estimate on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
proved in Lemma
2.6
.
∎

We can now prove a uniform invertibility estimate, which will be useful for the Chapman-Enskog approximation.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small, and let
\kappa_{0}>0
be fixed. Then for
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
and for any
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, the
operator
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}|_{\mathbb{V}^{1}_{\kappa}}
admits an inverse
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}:\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, with estimates

\displaystyle\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|z\right\|_{\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

for a constant
C
independent of
\epsilon
and
\kappa
.

Proof.

The existence of a set-theoretic inverse operator for
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}|_{\mathbb{V}^{1}_{\kappa}}
is immediately provided by the coercivity bound in Lemma
2.8
and the Lax-Milgram theorem, and the uniform bound in
\epsilon
and
\kappa
is provided by Lemma
2.8
.
∎

In the Chapman-Enskog expansion, it will be useful to also have the following invertibility result in an expanded space, which we prove using the extension of semigroup theory developed by Gualdani, Mischler, and Mouhot
[
58
]

[
34
]
.

Lemma 2.10

(Extension of the semigroup)
.

Let
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, and
\kappa_{0}>0
fixed. Denoting by
L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k})_{\mathrm{micro}}\subset L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k})
the subspace of purely microscopic functions, the operator
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}
is uniformly invertible on
L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k})_{\mathrm{micro}}
for all
k\geq 4
, with uniform bound

\displaystyle\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k})}\leq C_{k}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k})}

for some constant
C_{k}
independent of
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\kappa
.

When
\kappa=0
, invertibility was proved in Theorem 4.1 of
[
37
]
. Since the arguments for the lifted operator
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}
when
\kappa>0
are very similar, we defer the proof of this lemma to Appendix
A
.

2.4.
Kawashima compensators

To compensate for the lack of coercivity, we use the Kawashima compensator method, developed by Kawashima to prove energy decay on the whole space
[
42
]
, which is related to theories of hypocoercivity, as noted in
[
66
]

[
23
]
. The Kawashima construction involves finding a real skew-symmetric operator
K
of finite rank such that
[K,\xi_{1}]
will be coercive on the macroscopic term. This is useful for energy estimates. This method was crucially used to prove linearized a priori estimates for kinetic shocks for the hard sphere Boltzmann
[
56
]
and Landau equations
[
1
]
, as well as time decay for Vlasov-Poisson Boltzmann equation
[
24
]
. Before we can define the compensator, we first introduce the discretized subspaces

(51)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}=\operatorname*{Span}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left|\alpha\right|\leq N,\\
\alpha_{2}\equiv\alpha_{3}\equiv 0\ (\operatorname{mod}2)\end{subarray}}\xi^{\alpha}\underline{M}

in
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
, noting the inclusion
\mathbb{U}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
, and define

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}:{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{N}

as the associated orthogonal projection operator for any integer
N\geq 0
.

Lemma 2.11

(Lemma 5.1 of
[
42
]
)
.

There exists a skew-symmetric operator
\bar{K}:{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
, which is finite rank in the sense that
\bar{K}=\Pi_{3}\bar{K}\Pi_{3}
, and satisfies the coercivity estimate

\displaystyle\left\langle[\bar{K},\xi_{1}]f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq c_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}-C_{1}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}

for all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{3}
, for some constants
c_{1},C_{1}>0
. Furthermore, the operator
\bar{K}
satisfies estimates

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{K}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}},\qquad\left\|\bar{K}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

for a constant
C
independent of
\kappa
.

For completeness, we provide a proof.

Proof.

We write
A=\Pi_{3}\xi_{1}\Pi_{3}
and write it as a matrix in macro-micro coordinates

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}A_{00}&A_{10}\\
A_{01}&A_{11}\end{pmatrix},

noting the symmetry
A=A^{*}
. We then look for some

(52)

\displaystyle\bar{K}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta K_{00}&A_{10}\\
-A_{01}&0\end{pmatrix}

so that we can compute
\mathbb{P}[\bar{K},A]\mathbb{P}=\delta[K_{00},A_{00}]+2A_{01}A_{10}
, and we solve for
K_{00}
such that this expression is positive definite for some small
\delta>0
. Noting by construction that
A_{10}=(I-\mathbb{P})\xi_{1}\mathbb{P}
, we can explicitly compute

\displaystyle A_{10}\psi_{0}=0,\quad A_{10}\psi_{1}=\left(\xi_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\right)\underline{M},\quad A_{10}\psi_{2}=\frac{\xi_{1}\left|\xi\right|^{2}-5\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{6}}\underline{M}

where the second and third terms correspond to viscous and heat conduction moments. We can then compute the matrix

\displaystyle A_{01}A_{10}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&4/3&0\\
0&0&5/3\end{pmatrix}

in the orthonormal basis
\left\{\psi_{0},\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\right\}\subset\mathbb{U}
, which we see is only positive semi-definite, corresponding to the lack of dissipation in the momentum equation. Computing

\displaystyle A_{00}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&\sqrt{2/3}\\
0&\sqrt{2/3}&0\end{pmatrix}

in orthonormal coordinates, we then see that for

\displaystyle K_{00}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

we have that
\left\langle[K_{00},A_{00}]\psi_{0},\psi_{0}\right\rangle=2>0
. For
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathbb{U}
, writing
\mathfrak{u}_{0}=\left\langle\mathfrak{u},\psi_{0}\right\rangle
and
\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{u}_{0}\psi_{0}+\mathfrak{u}_{1}
,
we can therefore bound

\displaystyle\left\langle\Big(\delta[K_{00},A_{00}]+2A_{01}A_{10}\Big)\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq 2\delta\left|\mathfrak{u}_{0}\right|^{2}-2C\delta\left|\mathfrak{u}_{0}\right|\left\|\mathfrak{u}_{1}\right\|+\frac{8}{3}\left\|\mathfrak{u}_{1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq c\left(\left|\mathfrak{u}_{0}\right|^{2}+\left\|\mathfrak{u}_{1}\right\|^{2}\right)=c\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|^{2},

where the last bound holds for
\delta>0
sufficiently small, by computations of the discriminant. By similar matrix arguments, the first bound in the lemma is proved.

To prove uniform boundedness on
{\mathcal{H}}^{\pm 1}_{\kappa}
, we note that
K
maps into the finite-dimensional space
{\mathcal{H}}_{3}\subset\mathcal{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}
on which all norms are equivalent, so we can then compute

\displaystyle\left\|Kf\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|Kf\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

for a uniform constant
C>0
using Lemma
2.6
. The bound in
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
then follows by taking adjoints
K^{*}=-K
.
∎

We note that
K_{00}
can be constructed for much more general systems, by the theory of Kawashima
[
41
]
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small and
\kappa_{0}>0
fixed, and define
K=\delta_{1}\bar{K}
. Then there exist constants
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}>0
uniform in
\epsilon,\kappa
, such that for all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, the bound

\displaystyle\left\langle\Big(K\xi_{1}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\Big)f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta_{0}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

holds.

Proof.

We use by symmetry of
[\bar{K},\xi_{1}]
that
\Pi_{3}[\bar{K},\xi_{1}]\Pi_{3}
, and for
f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{3}
we have the uniform bounds

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{3}}

by Lemma
2.6
and by equivalence of the
\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
norms on the finite subspace
{\mathcal{H}}_{3}
. Therefore, using Lemma
2.8
, for
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
we can estimate

\displaystyle\left\langle\Big(K\xi_{1}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\Big)f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

\displaystyle\geq\delta\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}-C\epsilon\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\delta_{1}\left(c_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|^{2}-C_{1}\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\right),

and for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, there exists a uniform
\delta_{1}
such that this can be bounded below by
\delta_{0}(\left\|\mathbb{P}f\right\|+\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}})
. But the norms
\mathbb{U}\oplus\mathbb{V}^{1}_{\kappa}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
are uniformly equivalent for a constant independent of
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
by the construction of
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
and the open mapping theorem, and this proves the lemma.
∎

3.
The Chapman-Enskog approximation

We now justify the approximation of the Boltzmann equation by the compressible Navier-Stokes system (
23
), using the Chapman-Enskog expansion
[
15
]
. The derivation is classical, and has been done first for the Boltzmann equation with hard cutoff potentials
[
44
]
and later for more general kinetic systems
[
8
]
, however we will require uniform estimates in
\kappa
for the lifted problem to show convergence of the Galerkin scheme in the limit
\kappa\to 0
, so we carry out the explicit derivation. Similar uniform estimates were proved for the Landau collision operator in
[
1
]
. Introducing a time-dependence which will be useful for our derivation, we suppose we have a solution
F
to

(53)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F+(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}F=Q_{\kappa}(F,F),

and make the assumption of a hyperbolic scaling
\tau=\epsilon t
,
\zeta=\epsilon x
, which gives the rescaled equation

(54)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}F+(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{\zeta}F=\frac{1}{\epsilon}Q_{\kappa}(F,F),

so that we have a Knudsen number of order
\epsilon
. Making the symmetry assumption
R_{2}F=R_{3}F=F
as in Section 1 and defining the macroscopic part

U:=\mathbb{P}F\in\mathbb{U},

we introduce the uncentred micro-macro decomposition

\displaystyle F=M_{U}+f^{\perp}

for
f^{\perp}=O(\epsilon)
purely microscopic,
and we solve for
f^{\perp}
up to
O(\epsilon^{2})
order corrections, such that
F
solves (
54
). Using the cancellation
Q_{\kappa}(M_{U},M_{U})=0
, we can rewrite equation (
54
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}L_{U,\kappa}f^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}F+(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{\zeta}F+O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\tau}+(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{\zeta})M_{U}+O(\epsilon)

(55)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}M_{U}\partial_{\tau}U+(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}M_{U}\partial_{\zeta}U+O(\epsilon).

Since
L_{\kappa,U}f^{\perp}
is purely microscopic, we can take macroscopic moments of this equation, and writing
J(U)=\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}M_{U})
for the projected transport term, we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}\mathbb{P}F+\partial_{\zeta}\mathbb{P}\left[(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})F\right]+O(\epsilon)

(56)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}U+(J^{\prime}(U)-\mathsf{s})\partial_{\zeta}U+O(\epsilon).

Expressing
V=(\rho,u,T)
in hydrodynamic variables (
12
) by the identification of
\mathbb{U}
with the space of hydrodynamic quantities (
30
), and using the Maxwellian moment computations (
15
) and (
16
), we see that this can be expressed in conservative variables as

\displaystyle(\partial_{\tau}-\mathsf{s}\partial_{\zeta})\left[\mathsf{f}_{0}(\rho,u,T)\right]+\partial_{\zeta}\left[\mathsf{f}_{1}(\rho,u,T)\right]=O(\epsilon),

which are precisely the Euler equations (
1
) in a moving reference frame, up to an order
O(\epsilon)
correction. Plugging the macroscopic equation (
56
) back into the original equation (
55
) then gives us

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}L_{U,\kappa}f^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\left(-\mathrm{d}M_{U}(J^{\prime}(U)-\mathsf{s})+(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}M_{U}\right)\partial_{\zeta}u+O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{U}\right)(\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}M_{U})\partial_{\zeta}U+O(\epsilon),

where we have used the identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}M_{U}J^{\prime}(U)=\mathrm{d}M_{U}\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}M_{U})=\mathbb{P}_{U}(\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}M_{U}).

We can therefore define the
microscopic correction

\displaystyle b_{\kappa}^{\perp}[U]=L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}\left[\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{U}\right)(\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}M_{U})\right],

such that formally we have

(57)

\displaystyle f^{\perp}=f_{\kappa}^{\perp}[U]=b_{\kappa}^{\perp}[U]\,\epsilon\partial_{\zeta}U=b_{\kappa}^{\perp}[U]\partial_{x}U

up to an order
O(\epsilon^{2})
correction, and we take this as our definition of the Chapman-Enskog correction, and we write
f^{\perp}[U]=f_{0}^{\perp}[U]
for the Chapman-Enskog correction for the original equation (
3
). Plugging this into (
54
) and taking macroscopic parts, we can then expand to second order to get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\partial_{\tau}F+(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{\zeta}F)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}U+\partial_{\zeta}\mathbb{P}\left[(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})(M_{U}+\epsilon b^{\perp}[U]\partial_{\zeta}U+O(\epsilon^{2}))\right]

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}U+(J^{\prime}(U)-\mathsf{s})\partial_{\zeta}U-\epsilon\partial_{\zeta}(B(U)\partial_{\zeta}U)+O(\epsilon^{2})

where we define the diffusion term

\displaystyle B_{\kappa}(U):=-\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}b_{\kappa}^{\perp}[U])=-\mathbb{P}\left(\xi_{1}L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}\left(\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{U}\right)(\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}M_{U})\right)\right),

and write
B(U)=B_{0}(U)
for the original diffusion operator. We now compute the diffusion matrix precisely. Setting
\mathsf{V}=T^{-1/2}(\xi-ue_{1})
, we compute

\displaystyle b^{\perp}_{\kappa}[U]\partial_{x}U

\displaystyle=L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}\left(\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{U}\right)(\xi_{1}\partial_{x}M_{U})\right)

\displaystyle=L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}\left(\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{U}\right)\left[\xi_{1}\left(\frac{\partial M}{\partial\rho}\partial_{x}\rho+\frac{\partial M}{\partial u}\partial_{x}u+\frac{\partial M}{\partial T}\partial_{x}T\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}\left(\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{u}\right)\left[\xi_{1}M_{U}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}-u}{T}\partial_{x}u+\frac{1}{2T}\left(\frac{\left|\xi-ue_{1}\right|^{2}}{T}-3\right)\partial_{x}T\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}\left[M_{U}\left(\left(\mathsf{V}_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left|\mathsf{V}\right|^{2}\right)\partial_{x}u+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{T}}\mathsf{V}_{1}\left(\left|\mathsf{V}\right|^{2}-5\right)\partial_{x}T\right)\right]

\displaystyle=L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}\left[M_{U}\left(\Phi(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}u+T^{-1/2}\Psi(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}T\right)\right]

where we have defined the Burnett functions (cf.
[
25
]
)

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathsf{V})=\mathsf{V}_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left|\mathsf{V}\right|^{2},\qquad\Psi(\mathsf{V})=\mathsf{V}_{1}\left(\left|\mathsf{V}\right|^{2}-5\right).

We can then define the inverted Burnett functions

(58)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\kappa}\underline{M}=-\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}(\Phi\underline{M}),\qquad\tilde{\Psi}_{\kappa}\underline{M}=-\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}(\Psi\underline{M})

at the reference Maxwellian. For
U\neq{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\left|U-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, then we have the formulas

\displaystyle-L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}(M_{U}\Phi(\mathsf{V}))

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}T^{-\gamma/2}M_{U}\tilde{\Phi}_{T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa}(\mathsf{V}),

\displaystyle-L_{U,\kappa}^{-1}(M_{U}\Psi(\mathsf{V}))

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}T^{-\gamma/2}M_{U}\tilde{\Psi}_{T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa}(\mathsf{V})

for all
\kappa\geq 0
. For brevity, we write
\tilde{\Phi}_{T,\kappa}=\tilde{\Phi}_{T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa}
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{T,\kappa}=\tilde{\Psi}_{T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa}
.

Proof.

This will follow from the temperature scaling law

(59)

\displaystyle L_{U,\kappa}[f(\mathsf{V})]=\rho T^{\gamma/2}\left(\underline{L}_{T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa}f\right)(\mathsf{V}).

and the identity
M_{U}(\xi)=\rho T^{-3/2}\underline{M}(\mathsf{V})
, by inverting the operator
\underline{L}_{T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa}
and using the definition (
58
). To prove (
59
), we compute

\displaystyle L_{U,\kappa}[f(\mathsf{V})]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!\left(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{\gamma}+\kappa\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{2-2s}\right)b_{s}(\cos\theta)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\Big[M_{U}(\xi_{*}^{\prime})f(\mathsf{V}^{\prime})+M_{U}(\xi^{\prime})f(\mathsf{V}_{*}^{\prime})-M_{U}(\xi_{*})f(\mathsf{V})-M_{U}(\xi)f(\mathsf{V}_{*})\Big]\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}

\displaystyle=\rho\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!\left(T^{\gamma/2}\left|\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}_{*}\right|^{\gamma}+\kappa T^{1-s}\left|\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}_{*}\right|^{2-2s}\right)b_{s}(\cos\theta)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\Big[\underline{M}(\mathsf{V}_{*}^{\prime})f(\mathsf{V}^{\prime})+\underline{M}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime})f(\mathsf{V}_{*}^{\prime})-\underline{M}(\mathsf{V}_{*})f(\mathsf{V})-\underline{M}(\mathsf{V})f(\mathsf{V}_{*})\Big]\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\mathsf{V}_{*}

for
\mathsf{V}_{*}=T^{-1/2}(\xi_{*}-u)
, and
\mathsf{V}^{\prime},\mathsf{V}_{*}^{\prime}
defined analogously, and removing the
T^{\gamma/2}
factor from the integral then proves the claim.
∎

Using this lemma, we can then rewrite

(60)

\displaystyle b^{\perp}_{\kappa}(U)\partial_{x}U=\rho^{-1}T^{-\gamma/2}M_{U}\left[\tilde{\Phi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}u+T^{-1/2}\tilde{\Psi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}T\right],

so we can now compute the hydrodynamical quantities of
B_{\kappa}(U)\partial_{x}U
. We see in the mass equation that

\displaystyle\int B_{\kappa}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi=-\int\xi_{1}b_{\kappa}^{\perp}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi=0

since
b_{\kappa}^{\perp}(U)
is purely microscopic. Meanwhile, we can compute

\displaystyle\int\xi_{1}B_{\kappa}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-\int\xi_{1}^{2}b_{\kappa}^{\perp}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-T\int\left(\mathsf{V}_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left|\mathsf{V}\right|^{2}\right)b_{\kappa}^{\perp}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{T^{1-\gamma/2}}{\rho}\int\Phi(\mathsf{V})M_{U}\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}u+T^{-1/2}\tilde{\Psi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}T\right)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=T^{1-\gamma/2}\int\Phi(\mathsf{V})\underline{M}(\mathsf{V})\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}u+T^{-1/2}\tilde{\Psi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}T\right)\,{\rm d}\mathsf{V}

\displaystyle=T^{1-\gamma/2}\tilde{\mu}\partial_{x}u=\mu_{\kappa}(T)\partial_{x}u

for
\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{\mu}(T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa)
. Similarly, we can compute the energy

\displaystyle\int\!\xi_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle B_{\kappa}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-\int\frac{1}{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\xi_{1}b_{\kappa}^{\perp}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-\int\left(\frac{1}{2}T^{3/2}(\left|\mathsf{V}\right|^{2}-5)\mathsf{V}_{1}+T\left(\mathsf{V}_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left|\mathsf{V}\right|^{2}\right)u\right)b_{\kappa}^{\perp}(U)\partial_{x}U\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho T^{\gamma/2}}\int\left(T^{3/2}\Psi(\mathsf{V})+Tu\Phi(\mathsf{V})\right)M_{U}\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}u+T^{-1/2}\tilde{\Psi}_{T,\kappa}(\mathsf{V})\partial_{x}T\right)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=T^{1-\gamma/2}\left(\tilde{\mu}u\partial_{x}u+\tilde{\varkappa}\partial_{x}T\right)=\mu(T)u\partial_{x}u+\varkappa_{\kappa}(T)\partial_{x}T,

for
\tilde{\varkappa}=\tilde{\varkappa}(T^{1-s-\gamma/2}\kappa)
, so we see for the original equation
\kappa=0
, we have coefficients
\mu,\varkappa
varying as
T^{1-\gamma/2}
. We then get the diffusion matrix

(61)

\displaystyle B_{\kappa}(U)\partial_{x}U=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&\mu_{\kappa}&0\\
