0&\mu_{\kappa}u&\varkappa_{\kappa}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\rho\\
\partial_{x}u\\
\partial_{x}T\end{pmatrix}

in conservative variables, and we write
\mathsf{b}_{0}
for the matrix in (
61
), suppressing the
\kappa
where there is no risk of ambiguity. We note that the diffusion coefficients are independent of the density. We have the following uniform estimates on the microscopic correction and the diffusion matrix.

Lemma 3.2
.

The microscopic correction
b^{\perp}_{\kappa}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]\in\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
varies smoothly in
(\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa)\in B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0})\times[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small.

Proof.

Using (
60
), this reduces to showing that
\underline{M}\tilde{\Phi}_{\kappa},\underline{M}\tilde{\Psi}_{\kappa}\in\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
, with smooth dependence on
\kappa\geq 0
.
By Lemma
2.7
and the Lax-Milgram theorem in the modified
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}
norm, and the fact that
\underline{M}\Phi,\underline{M}\Psi\in\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
are microscopic, we get that
\underline{M}\tilde{\Phi}_{\kappa},\underline{M}\tilde{\Psi}_{\kappa}\in\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
with uniform estimates in
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
. To show
\underline{M}\tilde{\Phi}_{\kappa},\underline{M}\tilde{\Psi}_{\kappa}\in C^{\infty}([0,\kappa_{0}];\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2})
, we compute the derivative
\partial_{\kappa}\underline{L}_{\kappa}=\underline{L}_{s,2-2s}
and derive the identity

\displaystyle\partial_{\kappa}\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}=-\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}\underline{L}_{s,2-2s}\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}.

Using that
\partial_{\kappa}^{2}\underline{L}_{\kappa}=0
and arguing by induction, we derive

\displaystyle\partial_{\kappa}^{n}\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}=(-1)^{n}n!\left(\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}\underline{L}_{2,2-2s}\right)^{n}\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1},

and note that
\underline{L}_{2,2-2s}
is bounded
\mathsf{H}^{\ell+4}_{\ell+4}\to\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
and
\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{-1}
are uniformly bounded operators on
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}\cap\mathbb{V}
in
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
, for all
\ell\geq 0
. Since
\underline{M}\Phi,\underline{M}\Psi\in\mathsf{H}^{2+4n}_{2+4n}
for arbitrary
n\geq 0
, this proves the lemma.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

The diffusion matrix
B_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}})
is smooth for
(\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa)\in B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0})\times[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small.

Proof.

The proof is immediate from Lemma
3.2
, by continuity of the operator
\mathbb{P}[\xi_{1}\cdot]
on
\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 3.4

(
[
1
]
)
.

Fix the left reference state
V_{-}=(\rho_{-},u_{-},T_{-})=(1,0,1)
, and let
V_{+}=V_{+,3}(\epsilon)
be the unique state on the third Rankine-Hugoniot curve defined by (
17
) and (
20
), satisfying
\lambda_{3}(V_{+})=\lambda_{3}(V_{-})-\epsilon
for
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
sufficiently small, with shock speed
\mathsf{s}=\mathsf{s}_{3}(\epsilon)
(cf. Chapter 8 of
[
20
]
). Then there exists a family of solutions
\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}(x-\mathsf{s}t)
to the compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
23
) with boundary conditions

(62)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}(x)=V_{\pm},

which vary continuously in
C^{0}(\mathbb{R})
in
\epsilon>0
. Writing
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}=(I|_{\mathbb{U}})^{-1}\circ\mathsf{f}_{0}(V_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon})
for the
\mathbb{U}
-valued lift defined by (
32
), we have the uniform estimates

(63)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{k}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}(x)\right|\leq C_{k}\epsilon^{k+1}e^{-\epsilon\theta\left|x\right|},\qquad k\geq 1

and

\displaystyle\left|\mathfrak{u}_{\pm}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}(\pm x)\right|\leq C_{0}\epsilon\,e^{-\epsilon\theta\left|x\right|},\qquad x\geq 0

for constants
C_{k},\theta>0
independent of
\epsilon
. We can further assume that
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}^{\prime}(0)\right|\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{2}
for some constant
c_{0}>0
independent of
\epsilon
. Where there is no risk of ambiguity, we write
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}=\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}
.

We rewrite the equation for solutions
\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x,t)=\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x-\mathsf{s}t)
to (
23
) in coordinate-invariant form

(64)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\left[J(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})-\mathsf{s}\,\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right]+\partial_{x}\left[B(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right]=0,

which will be useful for later computations.

Proof.

Existence and uniqueness up to translation of smooth solutions
\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}(x-\mathsf{s}t)
to (
23
) with boundary conditions (
62
) was proved in
[
30
]
, for general smooth viscosity
\nu(T)>0
and thermal conductivity
\varkappa(T)>0
coefficients. The uniform estimates (
63
) were proved in Apppendix B of
[
1
]
for solutions to the general five-quantity Navier-Stokes system with three-dimensional velocity
u\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with endpoints
u_{\pm}=(u_{\pm,1},0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with uniqueness up to translation shown during the proof. But since this equation is invariant under the transformations
(u,u_{2},u_{3})\mapsto(u,-u_{2},u_{3})
and
(u,u_{2},u_{3})\mapsto(u,u_{2},-u_{3})
, we either have
e_{2}\cdot\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}}\equiv e_{3}\cdot\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}}\equiv 0
, or
e_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)=e_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x+C)
for some
C>0
by uniqueness up to translation, but this contradicts the boundary conditions, so the solutions constructed in
[
1
]
are in fact solutions to (
23
), and the bounds (
63
) are proved.

Continuous dependence on
\epsilon>0
in the
C^{0}(\mathbb{R})
norm and the bound
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS},\epsilon}^{\prime}(0)\right|\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{2}
for suitably chosen solutions up to translation are immediate consequences of the geometric construction in
[
30
]
.
∎

We can then directly deduce the following asymptotics on the Chapman-Enskog correction term.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small, then the Chapman-Enskog correction
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
defined in (
57
) satisfies the bounds

\displaystyle\left\|f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq C\left\|f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}\leq C_{k}\epsilon^{k+2}

for every
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the estimate (
63
) and from smoothness of the microscopic correction
b^{\perp}\in\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
proved in

3.2
.
∎

If we define the macroscopic entropy
s=-\log(T^{-3/2}\rho)
from the first law of thermodynamics, we have the following classical result, which will provide a useful reformulation of the compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
23
).

Lemma 3.6

(
[
45
]
)
.

Define the scalar function
\eta=-\rho s
and let
A_{0}=\nabla^{2}_{(\rho,m,E)}\eta
be its associated Hessian matrix in conservative variables. Then the matrix
A_{0}
is positive definite, the matrix
\mathsf{f}_{1}^{\prime}(\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime})^{-1}A_{0}
is symmetric, and
\mathsf{b}_{0}(\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime})^{-1}A_{0}
is non-negative definite.

This shows that the compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
23
) form a symmetric hyperbolic-parabolic system in the sense of Kawashima
[
41
]
. The proof in
[
45
]
proceeds by direct matrix computations, however it was shown in
[
46
]
that Lemma
3.6
can be directly derived from properties of the kinetic entropy (see also the exposition in
[
57
]
).

4.
Fixed point argument

We now define the explicit linearization of the problem. Assuming
F\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
is a solution to (
3
), we can write the second order Chapman-Enskog expansion

\displaystyle F=M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}+f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]+f,

and linearizing (
3
) around the lifted Navier-Stokes shock profile
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}
gives the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f=\xi_{1}\partial_{x}f-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f.

We can then re-express (
3
) as

(65)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f=\mathcal{E}[f],

for an error term which we compute as

(66)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}[f]&=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]-(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\left[M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}-f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\right]\\
&\qquad+Q(f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}],f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}])\\
&\qquad+Q(f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}],f)+Q(f,f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}])\\
&\qquad+Q(f,f).\end{split}

By the construction of the Chapman-Enskog correction
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
, we will see that the error term is of order
\epsilon^{3}
, and is purely microscopic. This latter fact will somewhat simplify the linearized a priori estimates in Section
5
and Section
6
, and is key for showing continuity of the problem in the limit
\kappa\to 0
once we construct a right inverse to
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}
for
\kappa>0
by a Galerkin scheme in Section
7
. Using these estimates, we will construct solutions to the original problem (
3
) by contraction mapping.
We summarize these facts in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
for sufficiently small
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Then for any
f,g\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
for
k\geq 2
, the error term is purely microscopic, and has bounds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{E}[f]\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}\leq C_{k}\left(\epsilon^{3}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}+\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}\right)

and

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{E}[f]-\mathcal{E}[g]\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}\leq C_{k}\left(\epsilon^{2}+\left\|f+g\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\right)\left\|f-g\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}.

for some constant
C_{k}>0
independent of
\epsilon>0
.

Proof.

To see that
\mathcal{E}[f]
is microscopic, we compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathcal{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\mathbb{P}\left[(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}-f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\right]

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\left[J(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})-\mathsf{s}\,\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}-B(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right]=0

since the shock profile
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}
is constructed to solve (
64
). But we can therefore re-express

\displaystyle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f^{\perp}[

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]-(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\left[M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}-f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\right]

\displaystyle=\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right)(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}-\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right)(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\left[M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}-f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\right]

\displaystyle=\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right)(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}],

by the definition of
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
in (
57
),
and we can then bound

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{E}[f]\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}\leq C_{k}\left(\epsilon^{3}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}+\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}\right)

by the estimates on
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
in Lemma
3.5
and the bilinear estimates in Corollary
2.4
, and the fact that
H^{k}_{\epsilon}
is a Sobolev algebra with uniformly bounded multiplication in
\epsilon>0
for
k\geq 1
. To bound the difference
\mathcal{E}[f]-\mathcal{E}[g]
, we compute

\displaystyle Q(f,f)-Q(g,g)=\frac{1}{2}\left(Q(f-g,f+g)+Q(f+g,f-g)\right)

and similarly by Lemma
3.5
and Corollary
2.4
we get the desired bound.
∎

Combining this error estimate with the right inverse constructed in Proposition
2.2
, we can now prove the main theorem by a contraction mapping argument.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We define the operator
\mathcal{T}[f]=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{\dagger}\mathcal{E}[f]
, and we see by the construction of the right inverse
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{\dagger}
that
\mathcal{T}[f]=f
holds if and only if
f
solves (
65
) and
\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=0
.
We show that
\mathcal{T}
admits a fixed point on
H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small. By Lemma
4.1
and the bound in Proposition
2.2
, we have the estimates

(67)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|\mathcal{T}[f]\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}&\leq C_{k}\left(\epsilon^{2}+\epsilon\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}\right),\\
\left\|\mathcal{T}[f]-\mathcal{T}[g]\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}&\leq C_{k}\left(\epsilon+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|f+g\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\right)\left\|f-g\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}},\end{split}

therefore
\mathcal{T}
is a contraction mapping on the subspace

\left\{f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}\,\middle|\,\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq 2C_{k}\epsilon^{2}\right\}

for
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}(k)
sufficiently small, and combined with the estimate on the Chapman-Enskog correction
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
in Lemma
3.5
, this proves existence of a solution
F\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
satisfying the first estimate in Theorem
1.1
.

To prove uniqueness up to translation in a larger set, we note that
\mathcal{T}
is also a contraction mapping in the larger ball

\displaystyle\left\{f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}\,\middle|\,\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq\epsilon/4C_{k}\right\}

for
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}(k)
sufficiently small, and therefore
F
is unique among solutions to (
3
) satisfying the estimate

(68)

\displaystyle\left\|F-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq c_{1}\epsilon

and the boundary condition

(69)

\displaystyle\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}F(0)=\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(0)}

for some small
c_{1}>0
.
If
\bar{F}
is a solution to (
3
) which satisfies (
68
) but not (
69
), then we wish to show that
\bar{F}_{a}(x,\xi)=\bar{F}(x+a,\xi)
satisfies the boundary condition (
69
) for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
, which would prove uniqueness up to translation. We write

\displaystyle\bar{F}=M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}+f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]+\bar{f}

and compute the derivative

\displaystyle\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}\partial_{x}\bar{F}(x)=\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\prime}(x)+\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}\partial_{x}\bar{f}(x)

where we have used the identity (
39
) and the fact that
f^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]
is purely microscopic. But by bounding

\displaystyle\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}\partial_{x}\bar{f}(x)\right|\leq\left\|\partial_{x}\bar{f}\right\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq C\left\|\partial_{x}\bar{f}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq Cc_{1}\epsilon^{2}

for a uniform constant
C>0
by Sobolev embedding and by assumption on
\bar{F}
and Lemma
3.5
, for
c_{1}>0
sufficiently small, we can use the nondegeneracy condition in Proposition
2.2
and the viscous shock asymptotics in Lemma
3.4
to bound

\displaystyle\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}\partial_{x}\bar{F}(x)\right|\geq\left(c_{0}-Cc_{1}\right)\epsilon^{2},\qquad x\in\left[-\frac{M}{\epsilon},\frac{M}{\epsilon}\right]

for
c_{1}>0
sufficiently small and some
M>0
. But since
F\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
implies continuity of the quantity inside the absolute value, it must therefore stay the same sign, and since (
68
) implies

\displaystyle\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\bar{F}(0)-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(0)})\right|\leq c_{1}\epsilon,

the identity
\partial_{a}\bar{F}_{a}(0)=\partial_{x}\bar{F}(x)
means that
F_{a}
must satisfy (
69
) for some
a\in[-M/\epsilon,M/\epsilon]
, proving the theorem.
∎

5.
Microscopic energy estimates

We now carry out the basic energy estimate on the linearized problem

(70)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f:=(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f=z

for
f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, where we assume a purely microscopic remainder term
z\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
. Since we can only construction solutions through the Galerkin scheme with the lifting parameter
\kappa>0
, we will require uniform estimates in
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
to prove existence for the linearized problem in Proposition
2.2
. We keep the assumption of a microscopic remainder term
z
for the rest of this section.

We can then define the twisted energy functional

(71)

\displaystyle E[f]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle\partial_{x}z,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle z,f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle Kz,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

for a parameter
\lambda>0
which we will choose later. This is the terminology used in
[
1
]
, where the
K
term allows us to exploit hypocoercivity arising from transport. To show coercivity of the energy, we compute the following.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small and
\kappa_{0}>0
fixed. Then
for
f\in H^{1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
and
z\in H^{1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
, and for
\lambda>0
sufficiently large (independently of
\epsilon,\kappa
) we have the coercivity estimate

\displaystyle E[f]\geq\delta_{0}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-C\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)

for constants
\delta_{0},C>0
independent of
\epsilon,\kappa,\lambda
.

Proof.

To prove the estimate, we compute

\displaystyle E[f]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle\partial_{x}\Big[L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f\Big],\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f,f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle K\Big[(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}\Big]f,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle\Big[K\xi_{1}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}\Big]\partial_{x}f,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f,f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle Q_{\kappa}(\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}},f)+Q_{\kappa}(f,\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}),\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle KL_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}-I_{3}-I_{4},

where we have used antisymmetry of the compensator
K
and collected terms. Using Lemma
2.12
and Lemma
2.8
we can bound

\displaystyle I_{1}+I_{2}\geq\delta_{0}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\right)-C\epsilon\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We now bound the error terms. Using Lemmas
2.1
,
2.6
, and
3.4
we can compute

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq\left\|dM_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}\left|\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)\right|\leq C\epsilon^{2}

for a constant
C
uniform in
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon,\kappa
. Combining this with Lemma
2.7
we gives

\displaystyle I_{3}\leq C\epsilon^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

for a uniform constant
C
. To bound the last term, we define the uncentred mircoscopic projection

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})f=\mathfrak{v}+(I-\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{v}+(I-\mathbb{P})\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\mathfrak{u}

such that
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f
by (
50
). Using Lemma
2.11
, we can then bound

\displaystyle\left\|KL_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

for constants
C
uniform in
\kappa,\epsilon
, using the norm bounds on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
from Lemma
2.6
, giving the bound

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+C\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

for a constant
C
independent of
\epsilon
, using the Maxwellian correction bound from Lemma
2.1
. Combining these estimates and absorbing the term
I_{3}
into the bound on
I_{4}
for
\epsilon
sufficiently small gives the estimate

\displaystyle E[f]\geq\delta_{0}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-C\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left(\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-C\epsilon\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}}

for arbitrary
\lambda>0
. Taking
\lambda
sufficiently large ensures that we can absorb the term
\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}
by the positive terms, proving the claim.
∎

We now prove the main energy estimate for non-cutoff Boltzmann.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
. If
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f=z\in H^{1}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
and
f\in H^{1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, then we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\leq C\left(\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\right)

for a constant
C
independent of
\epsilon
and
\kappa
.

Proof.

We combine the coercivity bound in Lemma
5.1
with the upper bound

\displaystyle E[f]\leq\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\lambda\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+C\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

to get the estimate

\displaystyle\delta_{0}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+C\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda\left(\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+C\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

and the result follows by Young’s inequality and multiplying the equation by
\epsilon
, using the identity
\epsilon\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}^{2}
.
∎

We have the following high frequency estimate.

Proposition 5.3
.

For
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f=z\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
and
k\geq 1
and
f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\partial^{k+1}_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}z,\partial_{x}^{k+1}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{k}\epsilon^{k}\big(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\big)\Big)

for constants
C,C_{k}>0
independent of
\epsilon,\kappa
.

To prove this, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

For
\epsilon,\kappa>0
small, we have uniform bounds

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C_{k}\epsilon^{k+1}

for all
k\geq 1
, for
C_{k}
independent of
\epsilon
and
\kappa
.

Proof.

By a Faà di Bruno type identity, we can compute that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{k}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}\\
\sum_{j=1}^{k}jm_{j}=k\end{subarray}}\nabla^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}:c_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\partial_{x}^{j}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right)^{\otimes m_{j}}

where the product operator is defined in the sense of tensor products, contracted with some tensor-valued coefficients
c_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}}
– this formula can be derived by induction on
k\geq 1
. We can then collect terms, and using the bounds on the shock profile
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}
in Lemma
3.4
we can bound

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\left\|M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\right\|_{C^{k}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0}),\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2})}\epsilon^{2m_{1}+\cdots+km_{k-1}+(k+1)m_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\epsilon^{k+1}

where we have used smoothness of the Maxwellian manifold from Lemma
2.1
. Comparing the
\mathsf{H}^{2}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
norms gives the result.
∎

We can now prove the proposition.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

We differentiate (
70
) to get

\displaystyle(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}^{k+1}f-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f=z+r_{k}

with a purely microscopic remainder term

\displaystyle r_{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{j}\Big[Q_{\kappa}\!\left(\partial_{x}^{k-j}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}},\partial_{x}^{j}f\right)+Q_{\kappa}\!\left(\partial_{x}^{j}f,\partial_{x}^{k-j}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right)\Big].

We can then apply the energy estimate in Proposition
5.2
to get the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{k+1}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{\kappa}^{1}}+\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{\kappa}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}z,\partial_{x}^{k+1}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{\kappa}^{-1}}+\left\|(r_{k},\partial_{x}r_{k})\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\right)

for a uniform constant
C>0
, and we can then bound the remainder term as

\displaystyle\left\|(r_{k},\partial_{x}r_{k})\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+\ell\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\\[1.5pt]
j\geq 0,\,\ell\geq 1\end{subarray}}{\Big\|Q_{\kappa}\!\left(\partial_{x}^{\ell}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}},\partial_{x}^{j}f\right)+Q_{\kappa}\!\left(\partial_{x}^{j}f,\partial_{x}^{\ell}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right)\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+\ell\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\\[1.5pt]
j\geq 0,\,\ell\geq 1\end{subarray}}C\epsilon^{\ell+1}\left\|\partial_{x}^{j}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{k}\epsilon^{k}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{k}\epsilon^{k}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right),

where we have used Lemma
5.4
to bound the
\partial_{x}^{\ell}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}
terms, and this proves the claim.
∎

6.
Macroscopic stability estimates

To close the energy estimates in Proposition
5.2
and Proposition
5.3
, we need to estimate the macroscopic remainder term
\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|
at lowest order. We do this by a linearized Chapman-Enskog procedure, and by using a stability result for viscous shocks, and close the following stability estimate.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
, for
\epsilon_{0},\kappa_{0}>0
sufficiently small. Then there exists a unit vector
\ell_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{U}
, such that for any functions
z\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
and
f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
, and
d\in\mathbb{R}
solving the problem

(72)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f=z,\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=d,

where
k\geq 2
, we have the uniform estimate

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C_{k}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left|d\right|\right).

The unit vector
\ell_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{U}
can be taken to vary continuously in
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
, and satisfies the estimate
\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\prime}(0)\right|\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{2}
for a uniform constant
c_{0}>0
.

The proof of Proposition
6.1
ultimately relies on the following fluid stability estimate, which we prove by rewriting the perturbation problem as a non-degenerate ODE, and computing explicit eigenvalue asymptotics.

Lemma 6.2
.

For
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, and for any
d\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a vector
\ell_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{U}
such that the problem

(73)

\displaystyle(B_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}+\mathsf{s}I-J^{\prime}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}))\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{h},\qquad\mathfrak{u}(0)\cdot\ell_{\epsilon}=d

admits a unique solution
\mathfrak{u}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
for any
\mathfrak{h}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{U})\cap C^{0}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{U})
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
. This solution satisfies the uniform estimate

(74)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}+\left|d\right|\right)

for
C
independent of
\epsilon
and
\kappa
. Furthermore, the vector
\ell_{\epsilon}
can be chosen to depend continuously on
\epsilon
, and such that
\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\right|=1
and

\displaystyle\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(0)\right|\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{2}

holds for
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
.

This was proved in
[
56
]
and
[
57
]
using abstract results from
[
53
]
, and later for general dissipative systems of Kawashima type
[
1
]
. Here we present an elementary self-contained proof for our specific problem, and derive quantitative estimates.

Proof.

The proof relies on an eigenvalue analysis of the equation, however this is complicated by the fact that the diffusion
B_{\kappa}
is degenerate, so we must first introduce an appropriate decomposition of the equation. Expressing

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathsf{v}&A\end{pmatrix},\qquad\left(\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-A^{-1}\mathsf{v}&A^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

where
\mathsf{f}_{0}
was defined in (
12
) and writing

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&b_{0}\end{pmatrix},

for the matrix derived in (
61
), we define the associated matrices
a=\mathsf{f}_{1}^{\prime}(\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime})^{-1}
and

\displaystyle b=\mathsf{b}_{0}(\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime})^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
b_{21}&b_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
-b_{0}A^{-1}\mathsf{v}&b_{0}A^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

such that (
73
) can be expressed as

(75)

\displaystyle\big[b(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}+(\mathsf{s}-a(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}))\big]w=h,\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathfrak{u}=d

where we have used a change of variables
w=I[\mathfrak{u}]
and
h=I[\mathfrak{h}]
to conservative coordinates as in (
30
), and where we have temporarily suppressed the dependence of the diffusion
b
on
\kappa
. Computing the inverse

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{0}^{\prime}(\rho,u,T)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
-\dfrac{u}{\rho}&\dfrac{1}{\rho}&0\\
\dfrac{\left|u\right|^{2}}{3\rho}-\dfrac{T}{\rho}&-\dfrac{2u}{3\rho}&\dfrac{2}{3\rho}\end{pmatrix},

we can then compute the matrices

\displaystyle a=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\[5.0pt]
-\dfrac{2}{3}u^{2}&\dfrac{4}{3}u&\dfrac{2}{3}\\[5.0pt]
-\dfrac{1}{6}u^{3}-\dfrac{5}{2}uT&-\dfrac{1}{6}u^{2}+\dfrac{5}{2}T&\dfrac{5}{3}u\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle b=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\[1.00006pt]
-\mu_{\kappa}\dfrac{u}{\rho}&\mu_{\kappa}\dfrac{1}{\rho}&0\\[10.00002pt]
\left(\dfrac{\varkappa_{\kappa}}{3}-\mu_{\kappa}\right)\dfrac{u^{2}}{\rho}-\varkappa_{\kappa}\dfrac{T}{\rho}&\left(\mu_{\kappa}-\dfrac{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}{3}\right)\dfrac{u}{\rho}&\dfrac{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}{3}\dfrac{1}{\rho}\end{pmatrix}

and we note that the ODE (
75
) is degenerate in the density coordinate of
u
. We then define the transform

\displaystyle P(\bar{V})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
b_{22}^{-1}b_{21}&I\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathsf{v}&I\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
-\bar{u}&1&0\\
-\dfrac{1}{2}\bar{u}^{2}-\dfrac{3}{2}\bar{T}&0&1\end{pmatrix}

for general hydrodynamic states
\bar{V}=(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{T})
, such that

\displaystyle bP^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&b_{22}\end{pmatrix},\qquad b_{22}=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\mu_{\kappa}}{\bar{\rho}}&0\\[10.00002pt]
\left(\mu_{\kappa}-\dfrac{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}{3}\right)\dfrac{\bar{u}}{\bar{\rho}}&\dfrac{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}{3}\dfrac{1}{\bar{\rho}},\end{pmatrix}

and define the transformed variables

\displaystyle\tilde{w}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{w}_{1}\\
\tilde{w}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=P(\bar{V})w=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
-\mathsf{v}w_{1}+w_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
m-\bar{u}\rho\\
E-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{u}^{2}+3\bar{T})\rho,\end{pmatrix}

the new source term
\tilde{h}=Ph
, and the transformed matrix
\tilde{a}=aP
, which we can compute as

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(\bar{V})=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{11}&\tilde{a}_{12}\\
\tilde{a}_{21}&\tilde{a}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{u}&1&0\\[5.0pt]
\bar{u}^{2}+\bar{T}&\dfrac{4}{3}\bar{u}&\dfrac{2}{3}\\[10.00002pt]
\dfrac{1}{2}\bar{u}^{3}+\dfrac{5}{2}\bar{T}\bar{u}&-\dfrac{1}{6}\bar{u}^{2}+\dfrac{5}{2}\bar{T}&\dfrac{5}{3}\bar{u}\end{pmatrix}.

Evaluating
\bar{V}
at
\bar{V}_{\mathrm{NS}}
,
we can then rewrite (
73
) as

\displaystyle(\mathsf{s}-\tilde{a}_{11})\tilde{w}_{1}-\tilde{a}_{12}\tilde{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{1}+O(\epsilon^{2}\left|w\right|),

(76)

\displaystyle b_{22}\partial_{x}\tilde{w}_{2}+(\mathsf{s}\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{v}-\tilde{a}_{21})\tilde{w}_{1}+(\mathsf{s}-\tilde{a}_{22})\tilde{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{2}+O(\epsilon^{2}\left|w\right|)

where for the error term we have used the fact that
[\partial_{x},P(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})]=O(\epsilon^{2})
by the asymptotics in Lemma
3.4
. We note that for
\epsilon
sufficiently small. the term
\mathsf{s}-\tilde{a}_{11}=\mathsf{s}-\bar{u}_{\mathrm{NS}}
is invertible,
by the asymptotics

(77)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{s}&=u_{-}+\mathsf{c}_{-}-\frac{\epsilon}{2}+O(\epsilon^{2})\end{split}

which we derive from (
22
),
and by positivity of the speed of sound
\mathsf{c}_{-}>0
. We note that this is exactly the nondegeneracy condition used in
[
28
]
to construct shocks for hyperbolic-parabolic systems of Kawashima type. Solving for
\tilde{w}_{1}
and plugging the resulting equation into (
76
) then gives the ODE

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\tilde{w}_{2}-\mathfrak{m}\tilde{w}_{2}=O(\left|h_{1}\right|+\left|h_{2}\right|)+O(\epsilon^{2}\left|w\right|),

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}=b_{22}^{-1}\left[(-\mathsf{s}\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{v}+\tilde{a}_{21})(\mathsf{s}-\tilde{a}_{11})^{-1}\tilde{a}_{12}-\mathsf{s}I+\tilde{a}_{22}\right],

which we will show has a large eigenvalue and an
O(\epsilon)
eigenvalue that crosses the origin. We explicitly compute

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}=b_{22}^{-1}\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{1}{\mu_{\kappa}}\rho&0\\[6.99997pt]
\left(\dfrac{1}{\mu_{\kappa}}-\dfrac{3}{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}\right)\rho u&\dfrac{3}{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}\rho\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\bar{T}}{\mathsf{s}-\bar{u}}+\dfrac{\bar{u}}{3}-\mathsf{s}&\dfrac{2}{3}\\[10.00002pt]
\dfrac{\mathsf{s}\bar{T}}{\mathsf{s}-\bar{u}}-\dfrac{2\bar{u}^{2}}{3}&\dfrac{5\bar{u}}{3}-\mathsf{s}\end{pmatrix}

for which we can compute

\displaystyle\det\mathfrak{m}=\frac{3\bar{\rho}^{2}}{2\mu_{\kappa}\varkappa_{\kappa}}\left((\mathsf{s}-\bar{u})^{2}-\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{2}\right)

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=-(\mathsf{s}-\bar{u})\bar{\rho}\left[\frac{2}{5\mu_{\kappa}}+\frac{3}{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}\right]+\frac{3\bar{\rho}}{5\mu_{\kappa}(\mathsf{s}-\bar{u})}\left[(\mathsf{s}-\bar{u})^{2}-\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{2}\right].

We define the small parameter

\displaystyle\hat{\epsilon}=\big(\mathsf{s}-\bar{u}\big)^{2}-\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{2}

where
\hat{\epsilon}>0
for supersonic flow and
\hat{\epsilon}<0
for subsonic flow, and when
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}=\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}
, using (
77
) and (
21
) we can compute
\hat{\epsilon}(\pm\infty)=\pm\mathsf{c}_{-}\epsilon+O(\epsilon^{2})
, and the uniform asymptotics

(78)

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\epsilon}(\pm\infty)-\hat{\epsilon}(\pm x)\right|\leq C_{0}\epsilon\,e^{-\epsilon\theta\left|x\right|},\qquad x\geq 0.

using the fluid shock bounds in Lemma
3.4
. We can then compute that the matrix
\mathfrak{m}
has eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{-}
with asymptotics

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}=-\frac{15\bar{\rho}}{(15\mu_{\kappa}+4\varkappa_{\kappa})(\mathsf{s}-\bar{u})}\hat{\epsilon}+O(\hat{\epsilon}^{2})

and

\displaystyle\lambda_{-}=-(\mathsf{s}-\bar{u})\bar{\rho}\left[\frac{2}{5\mu_{\kappa}}+\frac{3}{2\varkappa_{\kappa}}\right]+O(\hat{\epsilon}).

For
\epsilon
and
\kappa
sufficiently small, by smoothness of the coefficients
\mu_{\kappa},\varkappa_{\kappa}
from Corollary
3.3
, we have that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{-}
are distinct. Therefore we can diagonalize
\mathfrak{m}
, and construct a smoothly varying matrix
\omega
such that we have

\displaystyle p:=\omega^{-1}\mathfrak{m}\omega=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{0}&0\\
0&\lambda_{-}\end{pmatrix},

and writing
\tilde{w}_{2}=\omega z
gives the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{x}z-pz=O(\left|h_{1}\right|+\left|h_{2}\right|)+O(\epsilon^{2}\left|u\right|)=:g,

where we have used that
[\omega(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}),\partial_{x}]=O(\epsilon^{2})
, again by the asymptotics in Lemma
3.4
. We can then solve for the stable component by

\displaystyle z_{2}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\!e^{\int_{y}^{x}\!\lambda_{-}(w)\,{\rm d}w}g_{2}(y)\,{\rm d}y

and using that
\lambda_{-}(x)\leq-c_{0}<0
for some
c_{0}
, we have the bounds

\displaystyle\left\|z_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\leq C\left\|g_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}

and

\displaystyle\left|z_{2}(0)\right|\leq\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!e^{c_{0}y}\left|g_{2}(y)\right|\,{\rm d}y\leq C\left\|g_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}=C\sqrt{\epsilon}\left\|g_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}

for a constant
C
independent of
\epsilon,\kappa
. We now impose the boundary condition

\displaystyle\alpha z_{1}(x)+\beta z_{2}(x)=d

for some
\alpha\neq 0
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, giving the solution

\displaystyle z_{1}(x)=\frac{d-\beta z_{2}(0)}{\alpha}e^{\int_{0}^{x}\!\lambda_{0}(y)\,{\rm d}y}+\int_{0}^{x}\!e^{\int_{y}^{x}\!\lambda_{0}(w)\,{\rm d}w}g_{1}(y)\,{\rm d}y

for
x\geq 0
and

\displaystyle z_{1}(x)=\frac{d-\beta z_{2}(0)}{\alpha}e^{-\int_{x}^{0}\!\lambda_{0}(y)\,{\rm d}y}-\int_{x}^{0}\!e^{-\int_{x}^{y}\!\lambda_{0}(w)\,{\rm d}w}g_{1}(y)\,{\rm d}y

for
x\leq 0
but by applying (
78
), we get the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}+\left|d\right|\right)

for a constant
C
depending on
\beta
and
\alpha^{-1}
. But since the conjugation matrix
\omega
is uniformly continuous in small
\epsilon
and
\kappa
, we can find a
2
-vector
\tilde{\ell}
independent of
\epsilon
and
\kappa
such that
\omega(0)^{*}\tilde{\ell}
has first component uniformly bounded away from
0
in
\epsilon
and
\kappa
, and setting

\displaystyle\ell_{\epsilon}=c_{\epsilon}\left(I|_{\mathbb{U}}\right)^{*}P(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(0))^{*}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\tilde{\ell}_{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix},

up to a normalization constant
c_{\epsilon}
such that
\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\right|=1
and using the change of variables formula
w=I[\mathfrak{u}]
, we see that the estimate (
74
) holds, and
\ell_{\epsilon}
varies continuously in
\epsilon
.
To prove the last part of the lemma, commuting the derivative in (
64
) when
\kappa=0
gives

\displaystyle(B(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}+\mathsf{s}I-J^{\prime}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}))\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}=-\partial_{x}B(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},

but applying (
74
) and the asymptotics in Lemma
3.4
proves the claim for
\epsilon
sufficiently small.
∎

To prove Proposition
6.1
, we will need to control excess moments in the linearized Chapman-Enskog procedure, for which we use Lemma
2.10
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

To compensate for the macroscopic error term
\epsilon\left\|w\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}
, we use a linearized Chapman-Enskog procedure, as in Section 3. Taking uncentred microscopic parts of (
72
), we get the equation

\displaystyle(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f-z=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}

for the uncentred microscopic part
\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})f
, so we can compute

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}

\displaystyle=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}\Big((I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f-z\Big)

\displaystyle=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}\Big((I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}(\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\mathfrak{u}+\tilde{\mathfrak{v}})-z\Big)

\displaystyle=b^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}+r

with the microscopic remainder term

\displaystyle r=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}\Big((I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\Big[\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}(\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\mathfrak{u})+\xi_{1}\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\Big]-z\Big).

Taking macroscopic parts of (
72
) then gives that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})f=0,

but since
\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})
is a bounded operator on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
and since we have
f\in L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
by assumption, we can integrate this equation in
x
and compute that

(79)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})f=\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\left(\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\mathfrak{u}+\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right)\\
&=\big[J^{\prime}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})-\mathsf{s}\big]\mathfrak{u}-B_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}-\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}r),\end{split}

and by using Lemma
6.2
, it remains to bound the remainder term

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}r)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\xi_{1}L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}\Big((I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}(\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\mathfrak{u})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\left[\xi_{1}L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}\Big((I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\Big)\right]-\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}z)

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}-I_{3}.

We can bound the first term as

\displaystyle\left\|I_{1}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}\Big((I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}(\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\mathfrak{u})\Big)\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}(\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\mathfrak{u})\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{1}}\left\|\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right\|\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\leq C\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|

for a uniform constant
C>0
, where we have used uniform invertibility of
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x),\kappa}^{-1}
from Lemma
2.9
, the Maxwellian bounds from Lemma
2.1
, and the viscous shock asymptotics from Lemma
3.4
.

To bound the second term, we require a uniform bound on
\left\|(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}
. While
(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
is guaranteed by Lemma
2.6
for
\kappa>0
, this comes with a constant that is unbounded as
\kappa\to 0
,
so we instead use the extension of semigroup estimate in Lemma
2.10
to get the bound

\displaystyle\left\|I_{2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}\Big((I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\Big)\right\|_{L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{4})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{4})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{5})}\leq C\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}.

Finally, we can bound the last term as

\displaystyle\left\|I_{3}\right\|\leq C\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{-1}z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

by uniform invertibility in Lemma
2.9
, and applying Lemma
6.2
to (
79
) (
72
) gives the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\left(\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}+\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\right)+C\left|d\right|,

which for
\epsilon>0
and
\kappa\geq 0
sufficiently small, we

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\right)+C\left|d\right|

for a uniform constant
C>0
. Combining this with the energy estimate in Lemma
5.2
and the derivative estimate in Lemma
5.3
for
k=1
, we get the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\left(\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\right)

(80)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\left(\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left|d\right|\right),

but by computing

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=\partial_{x}\left[\mathfrak{v}+(I-\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})\mathfrak{u}\right]=\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}+(I-\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}-\,{\rm d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}(\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})(\mathfrak{u}),

we can uniformly bound

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+C\epsilon\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}\right\|+C\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|

using the viscous shock asymptotics in Lemma
3.4
and the Maxwellian derivative estimates in Lemma
2.1
, and plugging this back into (
80
) for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small (independently of
\kappa\geq 0
) gives the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq C\left(\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left|d\right|\right)

which implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-2}\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{-1}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sum_{k=0}^{2}\epsilon^{1-k}\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left|d\right|\right)

\displaystyle=C\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left|d\right|\right),

proving the claim for
k=2
.

To prove the result for
k\geq 3
, we argue by induction. Assuming the claim for the case of
k-1
, and summing with the bound from Proposition
5.3
for
k-1
gives the estimate

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-k}\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{-k}\left\|\partial_{x}^{k-1}v\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\lambda\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\frac{C_{k}}{\epsilon}\left(\epsilon^{1-k}\left\|\partial_{x}^{k-1}z,\partial_{x}^{k}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)+\lambda C_{k}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{k-1}_{\epsilon}}+\left|d\right|\right)

by using the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{H^{k-2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{H^{k-2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}},

where the parameter
\lambda>0
may vary, but for
\lambda
sufficiently large (dependent on
k
but not on
\epsilon,\kappa
) this gives the bound

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C_{k}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left|d\right|\right),

proving the proposition.
∎

7.
Galerkin approximation

In Proposition
6.1
we have shown uniform estimates for sufficiently smooth
f
and
z
solving (
72
). In this section we show that for any
z\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
, we can solve (
72
) for a unique
f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
. We proceed by a Galerkin scheme, as in
[
56
]

[
1
]
. More precisely, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\kappa>0
be sufficiently small, and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}(\kappa))
for
\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)>0
sufficiently small. Then for any
d\in\mathbb{R}
and
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
, the problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f=(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f=z,\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=d

admits a unique solution
f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
.

The proof of this proposition will occupy the rest of this section. We argue by proving similar a priori estimates to Sections
5
and
6
to a discretized problem, then explicitly constructing solutions by a stable-unstable dimension counting argument, and using uniform estimates to converge to the infinite-dimensional problem by compactness. We now introduce our notation. Using the discretized subspaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
and associated projections
\Pi_{N}:{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
defined in (
51
), we define the discretized transport term

\displaystyle A^{(N)}=\Pi_{N}\xi_{1}\Pi_{N}

and the discretized collision operators

\displaystyle Q^{(N)}_{\kappa}(g,f)=\Pi_{N}Q_{\kappa}(\Pi_{N}g,\Pi_{N}f),\qquad\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{(N)}=\Pi_{N}\underline{L}_{\kappa}\Pi_{N},

and we introduce the macro-micro decomposition

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}=\mathbb{U}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{N}

where
\mathbb{V}_{N}
and
\mathbb{U}
are orthogonal in the
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
norm. It will also be useful to define the dual space

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa,N}=({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{N})^{*}

whose norm may differ from
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
for large
N\gg 2
, where we have taken duals with respect to the
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
inner product. We would then like to reprove the uniform estimates in Section
5
and Section
6
for the discretized system, but since we no longer expect
Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})
to vanish for
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}\neq{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}
, we must construct a discretized Maxwellian manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0},\kappa}^{(N)}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
corresponding to equilibria for the discretized collision operator
Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}
, which we construct by an implicit function theorem argument.

First, we need to show that the discrete projections
\Pi_{N}
are well-adapted to the spaces
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
. To do so, we define the quantum harmonic oscillator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}:=\underline{M}^{-1/2}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}\underline{M}^{1/2},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}=-\Delta_{\xi}+\frac{1}{4}\left|\xi\right|^{2},

which is known to be a positive self-adjoint operator on
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
, whose eigenfunctions are the Hermite functions

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}=\frac{(-1)^{\left|\alpha\right|}}{\sqrt{\alpha!}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\underline{M},

which satisfy

(81)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}\psi_{\alpha}=\left(\left|\alpha\right|+\frac{3}{2}\right)\psi_{\alpha},

and form an orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
such that
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
are even (cf.
[
64
]
). We note in particular that the set

\displaystyle\left\{\psi_{\alpha}\ \Big|\ \alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3},\ \alpha_{2}\equiv\alpha_{3}\equiv 0\pmod{2},\ \left|\alpha\right|\leq N\right\}

forms an orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
for all
N\geq 0
. We note as an immediate corollary that we have the commutation relation

(82)

\displaystyle[\Pi_{N},\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}]=0

for all
N\geq 0
. The basis
(\psi_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
then allows us to measure regularity in the space
\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}
in the following way.

Lemma 7.2
.

For any
\ell\geq 1
and
f\in\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
, we have equivalence of norms

(83)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\ell}}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}\leq C_{\ell}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}.

Furthermore, the projection operators
\Pi_{N}
are uniformly bounded in
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
and converge to the identity strongly in
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
.

Proof.

The first bound is immediate by the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}
, so we prove the second bound. When
\ell=1
, writing
F=\underline{M}^{-1/2}f
, we can explicitly compute

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}F\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(-\Delta+\frac{1}{4}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\right)F,\left(-\Delta+\frac{1}{4}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\right)F\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\left(\left|\Delta F\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\left|\nabla F\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{16}\left|\xi\right|^{4}\left|F\right|^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}\xi-\frac{3}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\left|F\right|^{2}\,{\rm d}\xi,

and combining this with the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}\geq\frac{9}{4}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}

from the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}
has spectrum in
[3/2,\infty)
due to (
81
), the lemma is proved when
\ell=1
.
We now prove the general case. We first note the commutation relations

\displaystyle[\nabla_{\xi},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}]=\frac{1}{2}\xi,\qquad[\xi,\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}]=-2\nabla_{\xi},

allowing us to compute the identity

\displaystyle\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}F=\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}F+\sum_{\left|\alpha^{\prime}\right|+\left|\beta^{\prime}\right|\leq\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|}c_{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\xi^{\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta^{\prime}}F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

for arbitrary multi-indices
\alpha,\beta
and some coefficients
c_{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
. We now
assume by induction that the lemma holds for
\ell-1
, and using the norm identity
\|\cdot\|_{\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell}}=\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}\cdot\right\|_{H^{\ell}_{\ell}}
.
For any
\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq 2(\ell-1)
, we can then compute that

\displaystyle\left\|\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}F\right\|_{H^{2}_{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}F\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}F\right\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{\left|\alpha^{\prime}\right|+\left|\beta^{\prime}\right|\leq k}\left\|\xi^{\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta^{\prime}}F\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}F\right\|_{H^{2(\ell-1)}_{2(\ell-1)}}+\left\|F\right\|_{H^{2(\ell-1)}_{2(\ell-1)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}F\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell-1}F\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}F\right\|_{L^{2}}

where in the last line we have used the induction hypothesis and the coercivity estimate on
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{osc}}
in
L^{2}
, and summing over all
\alpha,\beta
such that
\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq 2\ell
proves the equivalence of norms.

To prove the strong convergence
\Pi_{N}\to I
in
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
, this follows immediately by norm equivalence, the commutation relation (
82
), and the fact that the spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
form a dense subset of
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
.
∎

For the rest of this section, we will use Lemma
7.2
as the definition of the
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
norm, by defining the inner product

\displaystyle\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}:=\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}f,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}g\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

for all
f,g\in\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
. We then have the following trilinear and coercivity estimates for the discretized collision operators.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
f,g,h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
, and let
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\kappa_{0}>0
sufficiently small, then we have the trilinear estimate

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(g,h),f\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}\lesssim C_{\ell}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,2\ell}_{\kappa}}\left\|g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,2\ell}_{\kappa}}\left\|h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,2\ell}_{\kappa}}

for all
\ell\geq 0
, and the coercivity estimate

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{(N)}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}

Using the equivalent norm
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}
for
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
defined in Lemma
2.7
, for any purely microscopic
z\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
, we also have the hypocoercivity estimate

\displaystyle-\left\langle\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{(N)}z,z\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}}\geq\delta_{\ell}\left\|z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1,2\ell}_{\kappa}}

for all
\ell\geq 1
, for some constants
C_{\ell},\delta,\delta_{\ell}>0
independent of
\kappa
and
N
.

Proof.

The trilinear estimate follows immediately by Lemma
2.7
and by re-expressing

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(g,h),f\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}=\left\langle\Pi_{N}Q_{\kappa}(g,h),f\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}=\left\langle Q_{\kappa}(g,h),f\right\rangle_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}

by orthogonality of the projections
\Pi_{N}
in the norm
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
norm defined in Lemma
7.2
, and similar arguments hold for coercivity and hypocoercivity of
\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{(N)}
.
∎

7.1.
Discretizing the Maxwellian manifold

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
\kappa_{0}>0
be sufficiently small, then for any
\mathfrak{u}
such that
\left|\mathfrak{u}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, there exist discretized Maxwellians
M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}

satisfying

(84)

\displaystyle Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)},M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)})=0,\qquad\mathbb{P}M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}=u

depending continuously on the parameters
M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}\in C_{u,\kappa}^{2}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0})\times[0,\kappa_{0}],\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12})
, and furthermore we have uniform convergence

\displaystyle\left\|M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}-M_{\mathfrak{u}}\right\|_{C^{2}_{u,\kappa}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0})\times[0,\kappa_{0}],\,\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12})}\leq C_{k}N^{-k}

for some constant
C_{k}>0
, for any
k\geq 1
.
Additionally, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|(I-\mathbb{P})\,{\rm d}M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left|\mathfrak{u}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|

for a uniform constant
C>0
.

To prove this, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
\kappa_{0}>0
be sufficiently small, then we have uniform convergence

\displaystyle\left\|Q_{\kappa}(\Pi_{N}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},\Pi_{N}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})\right\|_{C_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{2}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0})\times[0,\kappa_{0}],\,\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12})}\leq C_{k}N^{-k}

for all
k\geq 1
, for some
C_{k}>0
.

Proof.

To show this, we must first prove uniform convergence

(85)

\displaystyle\left\|\Pi_{N}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\right\|_{C^{1}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0}),\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12})}\leq C_{k}N^{-k}

for some
C_{k}>0
. Arguing generally, for any
\ell,k\geq 0
and
g\in\mathsf{H}^{2(\ell+k)+4}_{2(\ell+k)+4}
, by using the
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}
norm defined in Lemma
7.2
, the fact that
\Pi_{N}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}
commute, and the spectral decomposition in (
81
), we can compute that

\displaystyle\left\|(I-\Pi_{N})g\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}^{2}

\displaystyle=C_{\ell}\left\|(I-\Pi_{N})\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=C_{\ell}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\geq N+1}\left(\left|\alpha\right|+\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2\ell}\left|\left\langle g,\psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=C_{\ell}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\geq N+1}\left(\left|\alpha\right|+\frac{3}{2}\right)^{-4-2k}\left|\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell+k+2}g,\psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle\right|^{2}

(86)

\displaystyle\leq C_{k,\ell}N^{-1-2k}\left\|g\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2(\ell+k)+4}_{2(\ell+k)+4}}^{2},

but since
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\in C^{2}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0}),\mathsf{H}^{\ell}_{\ell})
for all
\ell\geq 0
by Lemma
2.1
, this proves (
85
). Using that
Q_{\kappa}(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})=0
, we can then derive by bilinear identities that

\displaystyle Q_{\kappa}(\Pi_{N}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},\Pi_{N}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})

\displaystyle=-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}(I-\Pi_{N})M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}+Q_{\kappa}\Big((I-\Pi_{N})M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}},(I-\Pi_{N})M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\Big)

and using the uniform bounds proved in Lemma
2.7
, combined with the derivative estimates in Lemma
2.1
and the uniform Sobolev bounds in Lemma
2.6
proves uniform convergence in
C^{1}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}
. To prove uniform convergence of
\kappa
derivatives, we note that
Q_{\kappa}
is affine in
\kappa
, so the result follows immediately by uniform convergence in
\kappa
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.4
.

We construct the approximate Maxwellians by an implicit function argument. Using the macro-micro decomposition
f=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
, we define the function

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})=(\mathfrak{u},Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v}))

which has derivatives

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(\underline{M})=(\mathbb{P},\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{(N)}),\qquad\mathsf{F}^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})=(0,Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v})),

and
\mathsf{F}^{\prime\prime\prime}=0
. By Lemma
7.3
, we have the uniform estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(\underline{M})^{-1}\mathsf{F}^{\prime\prime}(f)\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\big\|{Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(f,f)}\big\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa,N}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}

for a constant
C
independent of
\kappa
and
N
, therefore we can define the inverse

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}^{-1}(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})=(\mathfrak{u},G_{\kappa}^{(N)}(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}))

whenever
\left\|f-\underline{M}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq\epsilon_{0}
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, independently of
\kappa
and
N
(see Chapter XIV, Lemma 1.3 of
[
47
]
for estimates on the size of the neighbourhood for the inverse function theorem). For any
\mathfrak{u}
such that
\left|\mathfrak{u}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|<\epsilon_{0}
we define

\displaystyle M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}=\mathsf{F}^{-1}(\mathfrak{u},0)

which satisfies (
84
) by construction.

To prove uniform convergence to
M_{\mathfrak{u}}
, we note that for
g\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
such that
\left\|g-\underline{M}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}}\leq\epsilon_{0}
sufficiently small, independently of
\kappa,N
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(g)f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}\geq\delta_{\ell}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}

for all
\ell\geq 1
by Lemma
2.7
, by the uniform Sobolev bound in Lemma
2.6
, and the fact that
\Pi_{N}
form orthogonal projections on
\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}
. To bound the constant second derivative
\mathsf{F}^{\prime\prime}
, we use Lemma
7.2
to rewrite the trilinear estimate in Lemma
2.7
as

(87)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell-1}Q_{\kappa}(g,h),\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell+1}f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}&=\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}Q_{\kappa}(g,h),\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell}f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\\
&\leq C_{\ell}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell+1}g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell+1}h\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{osc}}^{\ell+1}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}},\end{split}

for a uniform constant
C_{\ell}>0
, but by uniform boundedness of the projections
\Pi_{N}
on
\mathsf{H}^{2\ell-2}_{2\ell-2}
, this implies

(88)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{F}^{\prime\prime}(h,f)\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell-2}_{2\ell-2}}=\leq C_{\ell}\left\|h\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell+2}_{2\ell+2}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell+2}_{2\ell+2}}

holds for all
\ell\geq 1
. We can therefore estimate

\displaystyle\left\|M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}-\Pi_{N}M_{\mathfrak{u}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathsf{F}^{-1}(\mathfrak{u},0)-\mathsf{F}^{-1}\left(\mathfrak{u},Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(M_{\mathfrak{u}},M_{\mathfrak{u}})\right)\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathsf{F}^{-1}\right\|_{C^{1}\left(B_{\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}}(\underline{M},\epsilon_{0}),\,\mathsf{H}^{6}_{6}\right)}\left\|Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}(M_{\mathfrak{u}},M_{\mathfrak{u}})\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}}\leq C_{k}N^{-k}

where the last bound holds for a uniform constant
C_{k}>0
, by Lemma
7.5
and boundedness of
\Pi_{N}
on
\mathsf{H}^{6}_{6}
. Using the derivative estimates in Lemma
7.5
and (
88
) then similarly proves

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}^{\alpha}\left(M_{\mathfrak{u},\kappa}^{(N)}-\Pi_{N}M_{\mathfrak{u}}\right)\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}}\leq C_{k}N^{-k}

for any
1\leq\left|\alpha\right|\leq 2
and for a uniform constant
C_{k}>0
, for
k
arbitrary, and together with (
85
), this proves all but the last estimate.

For the last estimate, differentiating (
84
) at
\mathfrak{u}={\mathfrak{u}_{-}}
gives that

\underline{L}_{\kappa}^{(N)}\,{\rm d}M_{{\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\kappa}^{(N)}=0,

but by the coercivity estimate in Lemma
7.3
this implies that
\,{\rm d}M_{{\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\kappa}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{U}
, and so the last bound follows by differentiability of
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}
.
∎

We then define the uncentred linearization

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}f=Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}\!\left(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)},f\right)+Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}\!\left(f,M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)

for
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, and we have the following discrete equivalent to Lemma
2.9
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small, and
\kappa_{0}>0
fixed. Then for
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
, any
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, and any
N\geq 3
, the operator
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}|_{\mathbb{V}_{N}}
admits an inverse
\mathbb{V}_{N}\to\mathbb{V}_{N}
with uniform estimates

\displaystyle\left\|\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}z\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}\leq C\left\|z\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}

and

\displaystyle\left\|\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

for purely microscopic
z\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
, for
0\leq\ell\leq 5
, and for a constant
C
independent of
\epsilon,\kappa
and
N
.

Proof.

From Lemma
7.4
and the fact that
M_{{\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\kappa}^{(N)}=\underline{M}
, we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]}\left\|M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}-\underline{M}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}}\leq C\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|,

so applying Lemma
7.3
gives the estimate

\displaystyle-\left\langle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}f,f\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}}\geq\left(\delta_{\ell}-C_{\ell}\epsilon_{0}\right)\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{2\ell}_{2\ell}}

for
f\in\mathbb{V}_{N}
, and taking
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small for all
0\leq\ell\leq 5
and applying the Lax-Milgram theorem proves the first estimate. Similarly arguing from Lemma
7.3
we have the estimate

(89)

\displaystyle\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\geq-\left\langle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\left(\delta-C\epsilon_{0}\right)\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2},

and taking
\epsilon_{0}>0
and inverting
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}
then proves the claim.
∎

As in the infinite-dimensional setting, we will define the uncentred projections
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}=\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\mathbb{P}
which satisfy the identities

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}f=L^{(N)}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\left(I-\mathbb{P}^{(N)}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\right)f

and
\mathbb{P}(I-\mathbb{P}^{(N)}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa})=0
, which are proved by differentiating the identities (
84
), and which also gives the useful reformulation of the microscopic projections

(90)

\displaystyle I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}=\left(\left.L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right|_{\mathbb{V}_{N}}\right)^{-1}L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}.

We now introduce the discretized linearized operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\eta,\kappa}^{(N)}=\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)\partial_{x}-\eta\partial_{x}^{2}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}

where we have introduced an artificial viscosity term
\eta\partial_{x}^{2}
for
\eta>0
. We solve the problem

(91)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\eta,\kappa}^{(N)}f=z,\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=d

for
d\in\mathbb{R}
and microscopic
z\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
. We have the following existence result for this problem.

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0}),\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
for
\kappa_{0}>0
and
\eta_{0}>0
sufficiently small, let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}(\kappa))
for
\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)>0
sufficiently small, and let
N\geq N_{0}(\epsilon)
be sufficiently large. Then for any purely microscopic
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
, the problem (
91
) admits a solution
f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
, which satisfies the estimate

(92)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C_{\kappa}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left|d\right|\right)

for a constant
C_{\kappa}>0
depending only on
\kappa>0
.

Remark 7.8
.

By elementary algebraic-geometric arguments, we see that
A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}
is an injective (and therefore invertible) operator if and only if
\mathsf{s}\in\mathbb{R}
is not a zero of the Hermite function
\psi_{N+1-2j}
for any integer
j\in[0,(N+1)/2]
. However, it is known that as
N\to\infty
the zeros of the Hermite functions
\psi_{N}
become dense in
\mathbb{R}
(Theorem 4 of
[
29
]
) and therefore the ODE without viscosity would be degenerate for a countably dense subset of shock strengths
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
. To avoid these technicalities, and to simplify the boundary value problem for the ODE, we introduce an artificial viscosity term
\eta\partial_{x}^{2}
for a varying parameter
\eta\in(0,\infty)
, which we will eventually take to be small. In particular, stable-unstable dimensionality computations will be much more straightforward in this context.

7.2.
Discretized stability estimates

In this subsection, we take
f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
and microscopic
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
satisfying (
91
), and write
f=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})
in the macro-micro decomposition. As for the problem (
72
), we can prove the following energy estimate.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
,
\eta\in[0,\eta_{0}]
, and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
\kappa_{0}>0
sufficiently small, independently of
N,\eta
, and
\eta_{0}>0
fixed. Then we have the estimate
