\displaystyle\eta\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\leq C\left(\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\right)

for a constant
C>0
independent of
\epsilon,\eta,\kappa
and
N
.

We have the following discrete equivalent to Lemma
2.12
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
\left|\bar{\mathfrak{u}}-{\mathfrak{u}_{-}}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
\kappa_{0}>0
sufficiently small. Then for all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
and
N\geq 3
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\langle\Big(KA^{(N)}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\Big)f,f\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\geq\delta_{0}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}.

Proof.

Noting that
[K,A^{(N)}]
is symmetric and satisfies
\Pi_{3}[K,A^{(N)}]\Pi_{3}
by the proof of Lemma
2.12
, we can apply Lemma
7.3
and the bounds on the approximate Maxwellian in Lemma
7.4
, and follow the rest of the proof of Lemma
2.12
, and this proves the claim.
∎

Proof of
7.9
.

Proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
5.2
. Using the energy functional defined in (
71
), we can compute

\displaystyle E[f]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle\partial_{x}\Big[\eta\partial_{x}^{2}f+L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x),\kappa}^{(N)}f\Big],\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle\eta\partial_{x}^{2}f+L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}f,f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle K\Big[(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}-\eta\partial_{x}^{2}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\Big]f,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle\Big[KA^{(N)}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\Big]\partial_{x}f,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}f,f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}\Big(\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)},f\Big)+Q_{\kappa}^{(N)}\Big(f,\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\Big),\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle KL_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}f,\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\eta\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\eta\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}\,{\rm d}x-\eta\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\langle\partial_{x}^{2}f,K\partial_{x}f\right\rangle\,{\rm d}x.

The last integral can be absorbed by the previous two integrals for
\lambda>0
sufficiently large, by boundedness of the operator
K:{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
. We can then use Lemma
7.10
, the trilinear and coercivity estimates in Lemma
7.3
, and differentiability of the approximate Maxwellians from Lemma
7.4
to derive the bound

\displaystyle E[f]

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{0}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\right)-C\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left(\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\lambda\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\eta\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\eta\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}\,{\rm d}x,

for
\delta_{0}
independent of all parameters, as in the proof of Lemma
5.1
. Using the bound

\displaystyle E[f]\leq\left\|\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\lambda\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+C\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

as in the proof of Lemma
5.2
, by absorbing the
\eta\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}
term by
\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}
by the norm bound in Lemma
2.6
and proceeding as in the infinite-dimensional case, the lemma is proved.
∎

We combine this with the macroscopic stability arguments to get the following.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
and
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
for
\kappa_{0}>0
and
\eta_{0}>0
sufficiently small, and let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}(\kappa))
for
\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)>0
sufficiently small. Then the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C_{\kappa}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left|d\right|\right)

holds for some constant
C_{\kappa}>0
independent of
\epsilon,\eta
and
N
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, we take uncentred microscopic parts of (
91
) and get the equation

\displaystyle\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)\partial_{x}f-\eta\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\partial_{x}^{2}f-z=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}f=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}

for
\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)f
.
Inverting the operator
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}
gives the expression

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}

\displaystyle=\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\Big[\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)\partial_{x}\left(\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\mathfrak{u}+\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\eta\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\partial_{x}^{2}f-z\Big]

\displaystyle=b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}]\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}+r

for

(93)

\displaystyle b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]=\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\Big[\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)A^{(N)}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\Big]

and

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\Big[\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\Pi_{N}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\,{\rm d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}(\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\mathfrak{u}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\Big[\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\Pi_{N}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad-\eta\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\Big[\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\partial_{x}^{2}f\Big]-\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}z

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}-I_{3}-I_{4}.

Defining

(94)

\displaystyle J_{\kappa}^{(N)}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}})=\mathbb{P}\left(\xi_{1}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)

and

(95)

\displaystyle B_{\kappa}^{(N)}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}})=-\mathbb{P}\!\left(\xi_{1}b_{\kappa,N}^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]\right)

and taking macroscopic parts of (
91
) then gives that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\mathbb{P}\left[(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\left(\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\mathfrak{u}+\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right)\right]-\eta\partial_{x}^{2}\mathfrak{u}.

Using that
f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}
by assumption, we can integrate this in
x
and re-express it as

\displaystyle\left({J_{\kappa}^{(N)}}^{\prime}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})-\mathsf{s}\right)u-B_{\kappa}^{(N)}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}=\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}r)+\eta\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}

where we have used that
\mathbb{P}A^{(N)}\Pi_{N}=\mathbb{P}\xi_{1}\Pi_{N}
by construction of
A^{(N)}
. Using Lemma
7.12
, we can rewrite this as

(96)

\displaystyle(J^{\prime}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})-\mathsf{s})u-B_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}=\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}r)+\eta\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}+O(N^{-1})\mathfrak{u}+O(N^{-1})\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u},

so it remains to bound the remainder term
r
. Using that

\Pi_{N}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\,{\rm d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}

is uniformly bounded in
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
by Lemma
7.4
, and by uniform invertibility of
L^{(N)}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}
in the
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
norm from Lemma
7.6
, we can bound

\displaystyle\left\|I_{1}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\left|\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}\right|\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\leq C\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|,

using the asymptotics in Lemma
3.4
. To bound the second term, we can use Lemma
2.6
to estimate

\displaystyle\left\|(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}},

which then gives the bound

(97)

\displaystyle\left\|I_{2}\right\|\leq C\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|

for a uniform constant
C
. The remaining terms can be bounded as

\displaystyle\left\|I_{3}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\eta\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}},\qquad\left\|I_{4}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq C\left\|z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}

for a uniform constant
C
using Lemma
7.6
, and combining terms gives the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|r\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left(\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|+\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\eta\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\left\|z\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}\right).

We can now apply the viscous shock stability estimate in Lemma
73
to (
96
), giving a bound

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}&\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\Big(\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left(\eta+N^{-1}\right)\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}+\left(\epsilon^{2}+N^{-1}\right)\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\eta\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\Big)+C\left|d\right|\end{split}

which reduces to

(98)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}&\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\Big(\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left(\eta+\epsilon^{2}\right)\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\eta\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\Big)+C\left|d\right|\end{split}

for
\epsilon
sufficiently small, and
N\gg\epsilon^{-2}
. To compensate for the
\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}
error term, we proceed as in the infinite-dimensional case and
differentiating (
91
) to get

\displaystyle(A^{(N)}\!-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}^{2}f-\eta\partial_{x}^{3}f-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\partial_{x}f=\partial_{x}z+Q^{(N)}_{\kappa}\!\!\left(\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)},f\right)+Q^{(N)}_{\kappa}\!\!\left(f,\partial_{x}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)

and applying the basic energy estimate in Lemma
7.9
gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{\eta}\left\|\partial_{x}^{3}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}z,\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)

where we have used differentiability of
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}
from Lemma
7.4
and the viscous shock asymptotics from Lemma
3.4
. Combining this with (
98
) and the original energy estimate in Lemma
7.9
gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{\eta}

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{3}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\sqrt{\eta}\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}^{2}+\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon\left\|\partial_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\leq C\Big(\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}z,\partial_{x}z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left\|z\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad+\left(\epsilon^{3}+\epsilon\eta\right)\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}+\eta\epsilon\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}+\epsilon\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left|d\right|\Big)

for some constant
C
independent of all parameters. We see that for
\eta<\eta_{0}=4/C^{2}
, the terms on the right-hand side involving
\eta
can be absorbed, as can the terms
\epsilon^{3}\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}}
and
\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}
for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small. Bounding

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left\|\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}(\partial_{x}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}})\mathfrak{u}\right\|+\left\|\left(I-\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|+\epsilon\left\|\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}\right\|

using the viscous shock asymptotics (
63
) and the discretized Maxwellian derivative estimates in Lemma
7.4
, the term
\epsilon\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}
can then be absorbed by the left-hand side, for
\epsilon\ll\kappa^{1/2}
sufficiently small, which proves the proposition.
∎

The proof of the uniform estimate (
92
) depends on consistency of the Chapman-Enskog matrices
(J^{(N)}_{\kappa},B^{(N)}_{\kappa})
arising from the Galerkin scheme, with the original hydrodynamic matrices
(J,B_{\kappa})
, and we show this in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.12
.

For
\kappa_{0},\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small, we have convergence

\displaystyle\sup_{\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]}\left\|J_{\kappa}^{(N)}-J\right\|_{C^{1}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0}))}\leq C_{k}N^{-k}

and

\displaystyle\sup_{\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]}\left\|B_{\kappa}^{(N)}-B_{\kappa}\right\|_{C^{0}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0}))}\leq CN^{-1}

for all
k\geq 1
, for some constants
C_{k},C>0
.
Furthermore, we have the uniform bounds

\displaystyle\left\|b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]\right\|_{C_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}^{0}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0}),{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa})}\leq C

for a constant
C>0
independent of
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
and
N\geq 3
, where the discretized Chapman-Enskog correction term
b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}
was defined in (
93
).

The convergence rate of the diffusion coefficients
B_{\kappa}^{(N)}
can be improved to
CN^{-k_{0}}
for any fixed
k_{0}
, though at the expense of a potentially smaller
\epsilon_{0}>0
. To see this, we note that super-polynomial convergence in the Galerkin scheme arises from results for convergence rates of the Hermite projections for Schwartz class functions, such as Maxwellian distributions, as in Lemma
7.4
. However our methods provide only finitely many velocity derivatives and moments for the Chapman-Enskog correction term
b^{\perp}_{\kappa}
in Lemma
3.2
, due to nonlinear estimates on the collision operator which may worsen at high regularity.

Proof of Lemma
7.12
.

Convergence
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\to M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\in C^{1}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
from Lemma
7.4
and boundedness of the operator
\mathbb{P}\xi_{1}
immediately gives the first claim, from the definition (
94
). To prove convergence of the diffusion coefficients, we prove the asymptotics

\displaystyle\sup_{\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]}\left\|b_{\kappa,N}^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]-b_{\kappa}^{\perp}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]\right\|_{C^{0}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq CN^{-1},

and by boundedness of the operator
\mathbb{P}\xi_{1}
, this will prove the second claim. Expressing

(99)

\displaystyle g_{\kappa,N}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)A^{(N)}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{u},\kappa}^{(N)}=\left(I-\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\mathbb{P}\right)\Pi_{N}\xi_{1}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}

and

\displaystyle g=(I-\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}})\xi_{1}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}

such that
b_{\kappa,N}^{\perp}=(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)})^{-1}g_{\kappa,N}
and
b_{\kappa}^{\perp}=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}g
, we can compute

\displaystyle\left\|\Pi_{N}\xi_{1}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}-\xi_{1}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Pi_{N}\left(\xi_{1}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}-\xi_{1}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\right)\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|(I-\Pi_{N})\xi_{1}\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}}

\displaystyle\leq CN^{-1}

uniformly in
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
, where the first term is bounded by uniform convergence of
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}
in
\mathsf{H}^{12}_{12}
from Lemma
7.4
and uniform boundedness of
\Pi_{N}
on
\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}
, while the second term is bounded using smoothness of the Maxwellians
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}\in\mathsf{H}^{8}_{8}
from Lemma
2.1
and the estimate (
86
). By continuity
\mathbb{P}:\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}\to\mathbb{U}
and by using uniform convergence
\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\to\,{\rm d}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}
in
\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}
by Lemma
7.4
, we get convergence

(100)

\displaystyle\sup_{\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]}\left\|g_{\kappa,N}[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]-g[\bar{\mathfrak{u}}]\right\|_{C^{0}_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}(B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0}),\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10})}\leq CN^{-1}.

We can then express

\displaystyle\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\!\!g_{\kappa,N}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}g

\displaystyle=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\Pi_{N}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\!g_{\kappa,N}+L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}\left(g_{\kappa,N}-g\right)

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

and we bound each term. The first term can be bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|I_{1}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{V})}\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\Pi_{N}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10},{\mathcal{H}})}\left\|\left(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}\cap\mathbb{V}_{N})}\left\|g_{\kappa,N}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\Pi_{N}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10},{\mathcal{H}})},

where
\mathscr{B}(E_{1},E_{2})
is the space of bounded linear operators
L:E_{1}\to E_{2}
on arbitrary Banach spaces
E_{1},E_{2}
under the operator norm and
\mathscr{B}(E):=\mathscr{B}(E,E)
, and
C>0
is a constant independent of
\kappa,N
, and
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}\in B({\mathfrak{u}_{-}},\epsilon_{0})
. Here we have used Lemmas
2.9
and
7.6
and equation (
100
). But we can then bound

\displaystyle\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\Pi_{N}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10},{\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(I-\Pi_{N})L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\Pi_{N}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10},{\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|\Pi_{N}Q_{\kappa}^{\prime}\left(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\Pi_{N}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10},{\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|I-\Pi_{N}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{6}_{6},{\mathcal{H}})}\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{H}^{10}_{10},\mathsf{H}^{6}_{6})}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\left\|M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}}-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{4}_{4}}

\displaystyle\leq CN^{-1}

where we write
Q_{\kappa}^{\prime}(g)=Q_{\kappa}(g,\cdot)+Q_{\kappa}(\cdot,g)
, and
we have used (
86
) to bound the projection
I-\Pi_{N}
and (
87
) to bound the collision terms, and Lemma
7.4
to estimate the difference of Maxwellians. The second term can be bounded as

\displaystyle\left\|I_{2}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq\left\|L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{-1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{V})}\left\|g_{\kappa,N}-g\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}\leq CN^{-1}

using the estimate (
100
) and uniform invertibility of
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}
on
\mathbb{V}
from Lemma
2.9
.

To prove uniform boundedness of
b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}=(L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}},\kappa}^{(N)})^{-1}g_{\kappa,N}
, we note that uniform bounds on
g_{\kappa,N}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
immediately follow from the definition (
99
) and from Lemma
7.4
, and by the uniform invertibility result of Lemma
7.6
this proves the claim.
∎

We would now like to explicitly construct our solutions. To do so, we do an eigenvalue analysis of the problem and compute stable and unstable dimensions.

7.3.
Eigenvalue computations

We now rewrite (
91
) as a first-order ODE. Using the macro-micro orthogonal decomposition
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}=\mathbb{U}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{N}
, we can re-express the operators

\displaystyle A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{00}&A_{01}\\
A_{10}&A_{11}\end{pmatrix},\qquad L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
L_{0}&L_{1}\end{pmatrix}

in matrix form, giving the macroscopic equation

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(A_{00}\mathfrak{u}+A_{01}\mathfrak{v})-\eta\partial_{x}^{2}\mathfrak{u}=0,

which can be integrated in
x
for
U,V\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, giving the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}=\frac{1}{\eta}\left(A_{00}\mathfrak{u}+A_{01}\mathfrak{v}\right).

Inserting this in the microscopic equation, we can then re-express the ODE in (
91
)
as

\displaystyle-\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{u}\\
\mathfrak{v}\\
\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\eta}\begin{pmatrix}A_{00}&A_{01}&0\\
0&0&\eta\\
\frac{1}{\eta}A_{10}A_{00}-L_{0}&\frac{1}{\eta}A_{10}A_{01}-L_{1}&A_{11}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{u}\\
\mathfrak{v}\\
\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
z\end{pmatrix},

which we rewrite in the form

(101)

\displaystyle-\partial_{x}\mathcal{F}+\mathbb{A}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{G},

for the variables
\mathcal{F}=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},w)^{\top}\in\mathbb{U}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{N}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{N}
,
and we define the endpoint matrices
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}=\mathbb{A}(\pm\infty)
. This ODE is asymptotically hyperbolic in the following sense.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
\kappa>0
be sufficiently small and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}(\kappa))
for
\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)
be sufficiently small, and let
N\geq N_{0}(\epsilon)
be sufficiently large. Then for any
\eta>0
, the matrices
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
have no eigenvalues on the imaginary axis
i\mathbb{R}
.

We note that there is no smallness condition on the artificial viscosity term
\eta\partial_{x}^{2}
– we will use this to compute stable and unstable dimensions of the matrices
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
for large
\eta>0
, and therefore for all
\eta>0
, by a perturbation argument.

Proof of Lemma
7.13
.

We argue by contradiction. We see that the eigenvalue equation

\displaystyle-i\tau\mathcal{F}+\mathbb{A}_{\pm}\mathcal{F}=0

is equivalent to the system

(102)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\mathcal{L}}f:=i\tau(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s})f+\eta\tau^{2}f-L_{\mathfrak{u}_{,}\kappa}^{(N)}f=0,\\
&\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb{U}}f:=\mathbb{P}(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s})f-i\eta\tau\mathfrak{u}=0,\end{split}

by replacing
\partial_{x}
with
i\tau
in the derivation of (
101
). To prove that (
102
) has no nontrivial solutions
f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{N}\otimes\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, we mimic the previous stability arguments from Proposition
7.11
in the spectral setting. For any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
we first define the associated energy

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{\tau}[f]:=\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\left(\tau^{2}-i\tau K+\lambda\right)\hat{\mathcal{L}}f,f\right\rangle=0,

for a parameter
\lambda>0
to be chosen later. Defining
\operatorname{Re}A=(A+A^{*})/2
for any operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
, we can compute that

(103)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\hat{\mathcal{L}}=\eta\tau^{2}-\operatorname{Re}L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\left(-i\tau K\hat{\mathcal{L}}\right)=\tau^{2}\operatorname{Re}\!\left[KA^{(N)}\right]-i\eta\tau^{3}K+\operatorname{Re}\!\left[i\tau KL_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right]

and collecting terms, we get that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{Re}\left[\left(\tau^{2}-i\tau K+\lambda\right)\hat{\mathcal{L}}\right]&=\eta\lambda\tau^{2}+\eta\tau^{2}\left[\tau^{2}-i\tau K\right]-\lambda\operatorname{Re}L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\\
&\qquad+\tau^{2}\operatorname{Re}\!\left[KA^{(N)}-L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right]+\operatorname{Re}\!\left[i\tau KL_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right].\end{split}

Applying this to (
103
), by using boundedness of
K
as a finite rank operator, and using the coercivity and trilinear estimates in Lemma
7.3
and the approximate Maxwellian bounds in Lemma
7.4
, we get the estimate

(104)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\eta\lambda\tau^{2}\left\|f\right\|^{2}&+\eta\tau^{4}\left\|f\right\|^{2}+\lambda\delta\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\tau^{2}\delta\left\|f\right\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\\
&\leq C\left(\eta\left|\tau\right|^{3}\left\|f\right\|^{2}+\lambda\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\left|\tau\right|\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left(\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|+\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)\right),\end{split}

for some uniform constants
C,\delta>0
, where for the last term we have used the bound

\displaystyle\left|\operatorname{Re}\left\langle KL_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}f,f\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\operatorname{Re}\left\langle KL_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}},f\right\rangle\right|

where
\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=(I-\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)})f
, but we can bound this by

\displaystyle C\left\|K\right\|\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left(\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|+\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\right)

by Lemmas
7.3
and
7.4
, and by using that
K
is finite rank. By taking
\lambda
sufficiently large (independently of all parameters), the terms
\eta\left|\tau\right|^{2}\left\|f\right\|^{2}
and
\left|\tau\right|\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}
in
104
can be controlled by the left-hand side, giving the bound

\displaystyle\eta\left(\tau^{2}+\tau^{4}\right)\left\|f\right\|^{2}+\tau^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\leq C\left(\epsilon\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}+\epsilon\left|\tau\right|\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\right),

but by Young’s inequality, this can be improved to

\displaystyle\eta\left(\tau^{2}+\tau^{4}\right)\left\|f\right\|^{2}+\tau^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}+\left\|\mathfrak{v}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}^{2}\leq C\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|^{2}\leq C\epsilon^{2}\left\|f\right\|^{2}.

By coercivity of the
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
norm in
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
and since
f\neq 0
, this then implies the bound

(105)

\displaystyle\left|\tau\right|\leq\frac{C\epsilon}{\sqrt{1+\eta}}.

To show that
\tau
cannot lie in this range, we use macroscopic estimates. Taking uncentred projections of the first equation of (
102
) gives

\displaystyle i\tau\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)f+\eta\tau^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}f=L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}

and solving for
\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}
gives

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}

\displaystyle=\left(L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\left[i\tau\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)\left(\,{\rm d}M_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\mathfrak{u}+\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right)+\eta\tau^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right]

\displaystyle=i\tau b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}[\mathfrak{u}_{\pm}]u+i\tau\left(L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\left[\left(I-\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right]+\eta\tau^{2}\left(L_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)^{-1}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}

\displaystyle=i\tau b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}[\mathfrak{u}_{\pm}]u+I_{2}+I_{3}

where the last two terms can be bounded as

(106)

\displaystyle\left\|I_{2}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\kappa^{-1/2}\left|\tau\right|\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}},\qquad\left\|I_{3}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\eta\tau^{2}\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

as in (
97
), and therefore we can bound

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}\leq C\left|\tau\right|\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|

for
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)
sufficiently small, where we have used uniform boundedness of the Chapman-Enskog correction
b^{\perp}_{\kappa,N}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
from Lemma
7.12
, and we note that this also implies
u\neq 0
by nontriviality of
f
. Applying this to the macroscopic equation of (
102
) then gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)\left(\,{\rm d}M_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}\mathfrak{u}+\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\right)-i\eta\tau\mathfrak{u}

\displaystyle=\left[{J^{(N)}_{\kappa}}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})-\mathsf{s}\right]\mathfrak{u}-i\tau B^{(N)}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})\mathfrak{u}-i\eta\tau\mathfrak{u}+\mathbb{P}\left(\xi_{1}I_{2}+\xi_{1}I_{3}\right).

Bounding the remainder terms
I_{2},I_{3}
by (
106
) and
using the error estimate for the hydrodynamic coefficients from Lemma
7.12
gives the equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left[J^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})-\mathsf{s}\right]\mathfrak{u}-i\tau\left[B_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})+\eta\right]\mathfrak{u}&=O\left(\left(\frac{1+\left|\tau\right|}{N}+C_{\kappa}\tau^{2}+\eta\tau^{3}\right)\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\right)\\
&=O\left(C_{\kappa}\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\right)\end{split}

where we have taken
N\gtrsim\epsilon^{-2}
. We now rewrite this system using the symmetric hyperbolic-parabolic theory of Kawashima
[
41
]
.
Expressing
W=(\rho,m,E)
in conservative variables and using the (smooth) symmetrizer
A_{0}
defined in Lemma
3.6
, we define
S=A_{0}^{-1}
, such that the matrices
\bar{A}_{\pm}=S(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})J^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})
and
\bar{B}_{\kappa,\pm}=S(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})B_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})
are symmetric, where we express
J^{\prime}
and
B_{\kappa}
in conservative variables, giving the reformulated equation

(107)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}\mathfrak{u}:=\left[\bar{A}_{\pm}-\mathsf{s}\right]\mathfrak{u}-i\tau\left[\bar{B}_{\kappa,\pm}+\eta S_{\pm}\right]\mathfrak{u}=O\left(C_{\kappa}\epsilon^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{u}\right\|\right),

and we wish to show that the operator
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
has inverse of order
\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\right\|\lesssim\epsilon^{-1}
, thus deriving a contradiction with nontriviality of
u
. We know as in the proof of Lemma
6.2
that
J^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})-\mathsf{s}
has two eigenvalues of order
O(1)
and a simple eigenvalue of order
\epsilon
, so by computing asymptotics of the determinants for
\epsilon
and
\tau
small and using that the matrices
\bar{A}_{\pm},\bar{B}_{\kappa,\pm},S_{\pm}
are real, this is proved for
(1+\eta)\left|\tau\right|\leq c_{0}
for some
c_{0}>0
sufficiently small. Meanwhile, whenever
\left|\tau\right|\eta\geq c_{0}\epsilon
, we can use energy estimates, exploiting symmetry of the operators (
107
) and coercivity of the term
\eta S_{\pm}
to prove
\left\|\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\right\|\lesssim\epsilon^{-1}
, but for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, one of these conditions must hold, proving the contradiction.
∎

We note a simplification compared to the analogous ODE hyperbolicity lemma proved in
[
1
]
. By more fully exploiting the lower order term
\eta\tau^{2}\left\|f\right\|
, we are able to improve from an
\left|\tau\right|+\eta\tau^{2}\lesssim\epsilon
bound to a
\tau\lesssim(1+\eta)^{-1/2}\epsilon
bound, removing the need for the Kawashima dissipativity theory of
[
63
]
.

We can now count the number of stable and unstable eigenvalues of this system. For any linear operator
A
, we have the stable eigenspace
\mathcal{S}(A)
corresponding to all eigenvectors with negative real part, and the unstable eigenspace
\mathcal{U}(A)
, corresponding to the eigenvectors with positive real part. Writing
r=\dim\mathbb{V}_{N}
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\kappa>0
be sufficiently small, let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}(\kappa))
for
\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)>0
sufficiently small, and let
N\geq N_{0}(\epsilon)
be sufficiently large. Then for any
\eta>0
, we have unstable and stable dimensions
\dim\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{-})=r+3
and
\dim\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{+})=r+1
.

Proof.

It will suffice to prove that

(108)

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{-})=r+\dim\mathcal{U}(J^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{-})-\mathsf{s}),\quad\dim\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{+})=r+\dim\mathcal{S}(J^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{+})-\mathsf{s})

from the eigenvalue computations in the proof of Lemma
6.2
.
Expressing
w=\partial_{x}\mathfrak{v}
in the ODE (
101
), we introduce the change of variables

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{u}\\
\mathfrak{v}\\
w\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}\\
\tilde{w}\end{pmatrix}=\tilde{\mathcal{F}},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}=(I-\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)})f=\mathfrak{v}+L_{2}^{-1}L_{1}\mathfrak{u}

where we have used (
90
), and where we rescale the variable
w
corresponding to
\partial_{x}\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}
by a factor of
\eta^{1/2}
, giving

\displaystyle\tilde{w}=\eta^{1/2}w+\eta^{-1/2}L_{2}^{-1}L_{1}\left(A_{00}\mathfrak{u}+A_{01}\mathfrak{v}\right).

We then express this as
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}=P\mathcal{F}
for the transformation matrices

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I&0&0\\
L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}&I&0\\
\eta^{-\frac{1}{2}}L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}A_{00}&\eta^{-\frac{1}{2}}L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}A_{01}&\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}I\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle P^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I&0&0\\
-L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}&I&0\\
\eta^{-1}L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}(A_{01}L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}-A_{00})&-\eta^{-1}L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}A_{01}&\eta^{-\frac{1}{2}}I\end{pmatrix},

so we can compute the matrix

\displaystyle\eta^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=P\mathbb{A}_{\pm}P^{-1}

\displaystyle=\eta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&I\\
0&-L_{1}&0\end{pmatrix}+\eta^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}A_{00}-A_{01}L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}&A_{01}&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}A_{01}+A_{11}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\qquad+\eta^{-3/2}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
R_{0}&R_{1}&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=:\eta^{-1/2}\left(\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}+\eta^{-1/2}\mathbb{A}_{\pm,1}+\eta^{-1}\mathbb{A}_{\pm,2}\right)

for second order terms
R_{0},R_{1}
which we do not make explicit, and taking
\eta\to\infty
, we consider this as an analytic perturbation problem in
\eta^{-1/2}
. We first compute the spectrum of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}
, but by the coercivity estimate (
89
), we can compute that the spectrum of
-L_{1}
lies in
\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,\operatorname{Re}z\geq\delta\right\}
for some
\delta>0
. To calculate the spectrum of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}
, using the strategy of
[
1
]
, we note that the matrix

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-L_{1}&0\end{pmatrix}

has square

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-L_{1}&0\\
0&-L_{1}\end{pmatrix},

and using the spectral mapping identity
\sigma(\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0}^{2})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0})^{2}
,
we get inclusion of the spectrum

(109)

\displaystyle\sigma\left(\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0}\right)\subset\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,\left|\operatorname{Re}z\right|\geq\sqrt{\delta}\right\},

so that the matrix
\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0}
is hyperbolic. Using the conjugation identity

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&-I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-L_{1}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&-I\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-L_{1}&0\end{pmatrix},

we see that the matrices
\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0}
and
-\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm,0}
are conjugate, so that we can compute the dimensions

\displaystyle\dim\ker\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}=3,\qquad\dim\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}\right)=\dim\mathcal{U}\left(\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}\right)=r=\dim\mathbb{V}_{N}.

We can now apply standard analytic perturbation theory near the semisimple
0
eigenvalue of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}
. Letting
P_{0}
be the projection onto the kernel of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm,0}
and writing
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
for the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm}
near
0
, using Theorem II.2.3 of
[
40
]
we derive the asymptotics

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{i}=\eta^{-1/2}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{(1)}+o(\eta^{-1/2}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq 3,

where
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{(1)}
denote the eigenvalues of the matrix

\displaystyle\tilde{T}^{(1)}:=\Pi_{0}\mathbb{A}_{\pm,1}\Pi_{0}

\displaystyle=A_{00}-A_{01}L_{1}^{-1}L_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\pm},\kappa}^{(N)}={J^{(N)}_{\kappa}}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})-\mathsf{s},

where we have used (
90
) and the definition of the approximate flux (
94
) to simplify this expression. By Lemma
7.12
, for
N\geq 3
sufficiently large, the real parts of the eigenvalues of this matrix are nonzero and have the same sign as that of
J^{\prime}(\mathfrak{u}_{\pm})-\mathsf{s}
so that (
108
) holds, proving the lemma for
\eta>0
sufficiently large, and by the hyperbolicity result of Lemma
7.13
, this proves the lemma for all
\eta>0
.
∎

7.4.
Existence for the linearized problem

We would now like to use the stable-unstable dimension calculations in Lemma
7.14
and the uniform estimates from Proposition
7.11
to construct solutions to (
91
), however we will first require the following lemma, on conjugation of non-autonomous equations. This was essentially proved in Lemma 2.6 of
[
55
]
, but we provide here a self-contained argument.

Lemma 7.15

(Conjugation Lemma)
.

Consider the ODE

(110)

\displaystyle u^{\prime}(x)+A(x)u(x)=h(x),\qquad x\in[0,\infty),\qquad u(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

for
A,h\in C^{1}([0,\infty))
, and suppose we have

(111)

\displaystyle\left|A(x)-A_{\infty}\right|\leq c_{0}e^{-\delta x}

for some matrix
A_{\infty}
and constants
c_{0},\delta>0
. Then for any
\theta\in(0,\delta)
, there exists an invertible matrix transformation
T\in C^{1}([0,\infty))
satisfying the bound

\displaystyle\left|T(x)-I\right|\leq c_{1}e^{-\theta x}

for some
c_{1}>0
, with
T(x)^{-1}
uniformly bounded on
[0,\infty)
, such that for the transformed variable
z(x)
defined by
u(x)=T(x)z(x)
, the system (
110
) is equivalent to the autonomous problem

(112)

\displaystyle z^{\prime}(x)+A_{\infty}z(x)=T(x)^{-1}h(x).

Proof.

To find a transformation
T
such that the formulations (
110
) and (
112
) are equivalent, we must solve the matrix-valued ODE

(113)

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(x)+A(x)T(x)-T(x)A_{\infty}=0

with asymptotic boundary condition
T(+\infty)=I
. We rewrite this problem as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=T^{\prime}(x)+[A_{\infty},T(x)]+(A(x)-A_{\infty})

\displaystyle=:T^{\prime}(x)+\mathcal{L}\,T(x)+\tilde{A}(x).

Choosing
\varkappa\in(\theta,\delta)
such that
\sigma(\mathcal{L})\cap(\varkappa+i\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
, and defining
\Pi_{-}
and
\Pi_{+}
as the eigenprojections on
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
corresponding to eigenvalues in

\displaystyle\left\{\mu\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,\operatorname{Re}\mu<\varkappa\right\}

and

\displaystyle\left\{\mu\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,\operatorname{Re}\mu>\varkappa\right\}

respectively, we can then define the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}[T](x)=I-\int_{0}^{x}\!e^{(y-x)\mathcal{L}}\operatorname{\Pi}_{+}\!\left[\tilde{A}(y)T(y)\right]\,{\rm d}y+\int_{x}^{\infty}\!e^{(y-x)\mathcal{L}}\operatorname{\Pi}_{-}\!\left[\tilde{A}(y)T(y)\right]\,{\rm d}y

by a standard hyperbolic variation of constants formula (cf. Chapter 9 of
[
65
]
),
and close the fixed point problem

(114)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}[T]=T,

which we see is equivalent to (
113
) whenever the integrals converge. We then show that
\mathcal{R}
is a contraction mapping on the set

(115)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\left\{T\in C([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})\,\Big|\,\left|T(x)-I\right|\leq c_{1}e^{-\theta x}\right\}

where
0<\theta<\varkappa
, and take
c_{1}>0
sufficiently small. For
T\in\mathcal{E}
we get the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{R}[T](x)-I\right|\leq\frac{Cc_{0}c_{1}}{\delta+\theta-\varkappa}\left(e^{-\varkappa x}+e^{-(\delta+\theta)x}\right)

and therefore, for

\displaystyle c_{0}\leq\frac{\delta+\theta-\varkappa}{2C}

we have that
\mathcal{R}
maps
\mathcal{E}
to itself. Defining the metric

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{E}}(T_{1},T_{2})=\sup_{x\in[0,\infty)}e^{\theta x}\left|T_{1}(x)-T_{2}(x)\right|

on
\mathcal{E}
and using linearity of
\mathcal{R}-I
, we then see that
\mathcal{R}
is a contraction mapping on the complete metric space
(\mathcal{E},d_{\mathcal{E}})
for

\displaystyle c_{0}\leq\frac{\delta+\theta-\varkappa}{4C},

so it remains to construct
T
without this smallness condition, but under the transformation
x\mapsto y+M
, the constant
c_{0}
will be replaced by
c_{0}e^{-\delta M}
, so that taking
M\geq 0
sufficiently large and solving (
113
) on the interval
[0,M]
then gives us our construction.

To prove invertibility of
T
, we see that
T^{-1}(x)
exists with uniform bounds for
x\in[M,\infty)
for
M>0
sufficiently large by the condition in (
115
), and by solving the equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\det T(x)=\det T(x)\operatorname{tr}\left(T(x)^{-1}T^{\prime}(x)\right)=\det T(x)\operatorname{tr}\left(A_{\infty}-A(x)\right),

uniform boundedness of
A-A_{\infty}
then proves the claim for all
x\in[0,\infty)
.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
7.7
, and explicitly construct solutions to the problem (
91
).

Proof of Proposition
7.7
.

We use the reformulation of the equation (
91
) as the first-order equation

\displaystyle-\partial_{x}\mathcal{F}(x)+\mathbb{A}(x)\mathcal{F}(x)=\mathcal{G},\qquad\mathcal{F}=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},w)^{\top},\quad\mathcal{G}=(0,0,z)^{\top}

defined in
(
101
), with the boundary condition
\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot U(0)=d
, and we wish to replace the matrices
\mathbb{A}(x)
with their endpoints
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
by applying Lemma
7.15
. We notice that the
x
dependence in
\mathbb{A}(x)
only arises from the collision term
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x),\kappa}^{(N)}
, so that the viscous shock asymptotics from Lemma
3.4
then give the bound

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{A}(-\left|x\right|)-\mathbb{A}_{-}\right|\leq c_{0}e^{-\epsilon\delta\left|x\right|},\qquad\left|\mathbb{A}(\left|x\right|)-\mathbb{A}_{+}\right|\leq c_{0}e^{-\epsilon\delta\left|x\right|},\qquad x\to\infty

for some constants
c_{0},\delta>0
. Therefore, by Lemma
7.15
there exist
C^{1}
uniformly bounded transformations
T_{-}
and
T_{+}
, defined on
[0,\infty)
and
(-\infty,0]
respectively, such that the change of coordinates
\mathcal{F}=T_{\pm}Z
and
\mathcal{G}=T_{\pm}H
on
\left\{\pm x\geq 0\right\}
converts the equation (
101
) to the equivalent autonomous problems

(116)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}Z_{-}(x)-\mathbb{A}_{-}Z_{-}(x)=H_{-},\qquad\partial_{x}Z_{+}(x)-\mathbb{A}_{+}Z_{+}(x)=H_{+},\qquad

where we must additionally impose the matching condition

(117)

\displaystyle T_{-}(0)Z_{-}(0)=T_{+}(0)Z_{+}(0)

so that the solutions
Z_{\pm}
can be glued at
x=0
, and the boundary condition

(118)

\displaystyle\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(T_{\pm}(0)Z_{\pm}(0)\big)=d.

We denote by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{\pm})
and
\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{\pm})
the stable and unstable subspaces of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
and define their associated spectral projections
\Pi_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{\pm})}
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{\pm})}
. We assume for now that the microscopic source term
z
, and therefore
\mathcal{G}
, are Schwartz-class in
x
, and using hyperbolicity of the matrices
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
from Lemma
7.13
, we see that the problem (
116
) has exponentially-decaying solutions of the form

\displaystyle Z_{-}(x)

\displaystyle=e^{x\mathbb{A}_{-}}Z_{0,-}-\int_{x}^{0}\!e^{(x-y)\mathbb{A}_{+}}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{-})}H_{-}(y)\,{\rm d}y-\int^{x}_{-\infty}\!e^{(x-y)\mathbb{A}_{-}}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{-})}H_{-}(y)\,{\rm d}y

\displaystyle=e^{x\mathbb{A}_{-}}Z_{0,-}-I_{-}(x)+I\!I_{-}(x)

for
x\leq 0
, and

\displaystyle Z_{+}(x)

\displaystyle=e^{x\mathbb{A}_{+}}Z_{0,+}+\int_{0}^{x}\!e^{(x-y)\mathbb{A}_{+}}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{+})}H_{+}(y)\,{\rm d}y-\int_{x}^{\infty}\!e^{(x-y)\mathbb{A}_{+}}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{+})}H_{+}(y)\,{\rm d}y

\displaystyle=e^{x\mathbb{A}_{+}}Z_{0,+}+I_{+}(x)-I\!I_{+}(x)

for
x\geq 0
for some
(Z_{0,-},Z_{0,+})\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{-})\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{+})
. Writing
I\!I_{0,\pm}=I\!I_{\pm}(0)
, we see that the matching and boundary conditions (
117
) (
118
) are equivalent to the conditions

(119)

\displaystyle T_{+}(0)\left(Z_{0,+}-I\!I_{0,+}\right)-T_{-}(0)\left(Z_{0,-}+I\!I_{0,-}\right)=0

and

(120)

\displaystyle\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}T_{+}(0)Z_{+,0}=d+\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}T_{+}(0)I\!I_{+,0}.

Solving for
(Z_{0,+},Z_{0,-})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{+})\times\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{-})
satisfying these conditions, we observe that these impose a
3+2r+1
-dimensional constraint on
(Z_{0,+},Z_{0,-})
, but by Lemma
7.14
we have the dimension

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{+})\times\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{A}_{-})=3+2r+1,

so it remains to show that this problem is non-degenerate in
(Z_{0,+},Z_{0,-})
. If we assume that
z=0
and
d=0
so that the linear equations (
119
) and (
120
) become homogeneous, we have by the stability theory in Proposition
7.11
that
f=0
and therefore
Z_{\pm}=0
and
Z_{0,\pm}=0
, so that the problem (
91
) can be solved for any
d\in\mathbb{R}
and any Schwartz class
z\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{V}_{N})
. We can then use Proposition
7.11
to get the estimates (
92
), and we extend to
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
by continuity.
∎

7.5.
Convergence by compactness

It now remains to show convergence of the Galerkin scheme to the original problem.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

We first construct
f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
for source terms
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}_{N_{0}}
for
N_{0}
arbitrarily large. By Proposition
7.7
, we have a solution
f_{N,\eta}\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
to the problem

(121)

\displaystyle\left(A^{(N)}-\mathsf{s}\right)\partial_{x}f_{N,\eta}-\eta\partial_{x}^{2}f_{N,\eta}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}f_{N,\eta}=z,\quad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f_{N,\eta}(0)=d,

and using the uniform estimate (
92
) we can then take a weakly converging subsequence
f_{N,\eta}\rightharpoonup f
as
N\to\infty
and
\eta\to 0
, converging in the space
H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
. The boundary condition
\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=d
is easily seen to hold by weak convergence in
H^{1}_{\epsilon}
, so it remains to show convergence for the rest of the equation. By applying the finite projection
\Pi_{N_{1}}
for
N_{1}\geq N_{0}
to (
121
) we get the equation

\displaystyle\Pi_{N_{1}}\left(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s}\right)\partial_{x}f_{N,\eta}

\displaystyle-\eta\Pi_{N_{1}}\partial_{x}^{2}f_{N,\eta}

\displaystyle-\Pi_{N_{1}}\left[Q_{\kappa}\left(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)},f_{N,\eta}\right)+Q_{\kappa}\left(f_{N,\eta},M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\right)\right]=z.

The first two terms are easily seen to converge weakly in
L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
, so it remains to show convergence of the collision term. But since
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}^{(N)}\to M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}
strongly in
L^{\infty}_{x}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
by Lemma
7.4
and
\Pi_{N_{1}}Q_{\kappa}
is a bilinear operator mapping to a finite-dimensional space, the trilinear estimate from Lemma
7.3
and standard results on products of weakly and strongly converging subsequences are enough to show that
f
solves
\Pi_{N_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f=z
for all
N_{1}\geq N_{0}
. Using that
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
by Lemma
2.7
and boundedness of
\xi_{1}:{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}
, we can take limits in
\mathsf{H}^{-2}_{-2}
as
N_{1}\to\infty
, proving that
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f=z
.
To prove the result for general
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
, we use boundedness of the inverted operator

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa},\left.\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}\right|_{x=0}\right)^{-1}:\Big(H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}_{N_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\subset\Big(H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\to H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}

by Proposition
6.1
and density of the discretized spaces
\mathbb{V}_{N_{0}}\subset\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
as
N_{0}\to\infty
, and extend by continuity.
∎

We would now like to take the limit
\kappa\to 0
using the uniform estimates proved in Proposition
6.1
, but we must first remove the dependence of
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}(\kappa)
on
\kappa.
To do this, we use the continuity lemma developed in
[
1
]
.

Lemma 7.16

(Lemma 5.11 of
[
1
]
)
.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are Banach spaces, and let
A(t):[0,1]\to B(X,Y)
be a norm-continuous family of bounded linear operators. Suppose we have a uniform coercivity estimate

\displaystyle\left\|x\right\|_{X}\leq C\left\|A(t)x\right\|_{Y}

for all
t\in[0,1]
and
x\in X
. Then if
A(0)
is surjective,
A(t)
is surjective for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Using this lemma and our previous uniform estimates, we can extend the range of
\epsilon>0
to be uniform in
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
, with the following lemma.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
for
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
\kappa_{0}>0
sufficiently small. For any
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
, the problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}f=z,\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=d

admits a unique solution
f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
.

Proof.

We follow the strategy of
[
1
]
. We first define the spaces

\displaystyle X_{\mathsf{s}}=\left\{f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}\,:\,(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa},\ \mathbb{P}(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})f=0\right\}

and

\displaystyle Y=H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}\times\mathbb{R},

where we use that the norms
H^{2}_{\epsilon}
are equivalent for
\epsilon\in(\epsilon_{0}^{\prime},\epsilon_{0})
for
0<\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}<\epsilon_{0}
.
Defining the shift operator
\tau^{\mathsf{s}}f(x,\xi)=f(x,\xi+\mathsf{s}e_{1})
, we can set

\displaystyle A(\epsilon)=\left(\tau^{\mathsf{s}}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon,\kappa}\tau^{-\mathsf{s}},\left.\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}\right|_{x=0}\circ\tau^{-\mathsf{s}}\right),

and by re-expressing

\displaystyle\tau^{\mathsf{s}}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\kappa}\tau^{-\mathsf{s}}f=\xi_{1}\partial_{x}f-Q_{\kappa}(\tau^{\mathsf{s}}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}},f)-Q_{\kappa}(f,\tau^{\mathsf{s}}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}),

we see that
A(\epsilon)
is bounded
X_{0}\to Y
by Lemma
2.7
. Continuity of the shock speed
\mathsf{s}
in
\epsilon
is standard (cf. Theorem 5.14 of
[
39
]
), while continuity of
\ell_{\epsilon}
was proved in Lemma
6.2
, and continuity of
M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}
follows from continuity in
\epsilon
of
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}
, from Lemma
3.4
, so we see that
A(\epsilon):X_{0}\to Y
is a norm-continuous family of operators. By Proposition
6.1
we have the uniform bound

\displaystyle\left\|\tau^{\mathsf{s}}f\right\|_{X_{0}}\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\left\|A(\epsilon)\tau^{\mathsf{s}}f\right\|_{Y}

for all
f\in X_{\mathsf{s}}
and for small
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
independently of
\kappa
, therefore by Lemma
7.17
, the operator
A(\epsilon)
is invertible for this range, proving the lemma.
∎

We can now prove the main proposition of the article.

Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

We first show that we can solve the problem

(122)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f=(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f=z,\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f(0)=d

for
z\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}_{\kappa}
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
, by taking limits
\kappa\to 0
in Lemma
7.17
. If we let
f_{\kappa}\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}
solve

\displaystyle(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f_{\kappa}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f_{\kappa}=z

where we use that
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}\supset{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
by construction, we have uniform bounds

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\kappa}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq\left\|f_{\kappa}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+C\left|d\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\left\|z\right\|_{H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}+C\left|d\right|

by Proposition
6.1
, we can take a weakly converging subsequence
f_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup f
in
H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
.
We immediately have convergence
\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f_{\kappa}(0)\to d
, as well as convergence
(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f_{\kappa}\to(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi})
in the space of distributions. To show convergence of the remaining term, we write out

(123)

\displaystyle L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}},\kappa}f_{\kappa}=L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f_{\kappa}+\kappa\left(Q_{s,2-2s}(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}},f_{\kappa})+Q_{s,2-2s}(f_{\kappa},M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}})\right)

but since

\displaystyle\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}Q_{s,2-2s}(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}},f_{\kappa})\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}+\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}Q_{s,2-2s}(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}},f_{\kappa})\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq C\left\|\underline{M}^{-1/2}f_{\kappa}\right\|_{N^{s,2-2s}}\leq C\kappa^{-1/2}\left\|f_{\kappa}\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\kappa}},

using (
49
), we get that the second term in (
123
) is of order
\kappa^{1/2}
and is therefore negligible, while we will have weak convergence
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f
in
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
by continuity of the operator
L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}
, and therefore we have a solution
f\in H^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
to (
122
).

Assuming now that
z\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{V}^{-1}
for some
k\geq 3
, we show that
f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
using the uniform estimates in Proposition (
6.1
) and a spatial regularity bootstrap. We let
\eta(x)\geq 0
be smooth and symmetric with compact support and defining the mollifiers
\eta_{\delta}(x)=\delta^{-1}\eta(x/\delta)
. Assuming by induction that
f\in H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
, we define the spatial mollification
f_{\delta}=\eta_{\delta}*f
, by convolving (
122
) with
\eta_{\delta}
we get the equation

\displaystyle(\xi_{1}-\mathsf{s})\partial_{x}f_{\delta}-L_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}}f_{\delta}=z+\mathcal{R}

with microscopic remainder term

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\Big[Q\left(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(y)}-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)},f(y)\right)+Q\left(f(y),M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(y)}-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}\right)\Big]\eta_{\delta}(x-y)\,{\rm d}y,

and we would like to bound this uniformly in
H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
as
\delta\to 0
. Investigating the effect of
\partial_{x}
-derivatives on this remainder, we note that if
\eta_{\delta}
has more than one derivative, then we can interchange the spatial integral with the integral in the definition of the collision operator and integrate by parts in
y
, so it will suffice to bound

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{j}M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}\eta_{\delta}(x-y)\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq C_{k}\delta^{-1}1_{\left|x-y\right|\leq C\delta}

for
1\leq j\leq k
, and

\displaystyle\left\|\left(M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(y)}-M_{\bar{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}\right)\eta_{\delta}(x-y)\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq C1_{\left|x-y\right|\leq C\delta},

using smoothness of the Maxwellian manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon_{0}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
from Lemma
2.1
and the uniform viscous shock bounds from Lemma
3.4
, giving the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{k}\mathcal{R}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}\leq C_{k}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k-1}},

where we ignore the dependence of the constant
C_{k}
on
\epsilon
.
Applying this to Proposition
6.1
, we get the bound

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\delta}\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}\leq C_{k}\left(\left\|z\right\|_{H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\kappa}}+\left|\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f_{\delta}(0)\right|\right)

but since
f\in H^{1}_{\epsilon}
is continuous in
x
, the remainder term
\ell_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathbb{P}f_{\delta}(0)
is uniformly bounded, so by taking a weakly converging subsequence
f_{\delta}\rightharpoonup f
in
H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
and using that
f_{\delta}\to f
in
H^{k-1}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
, we get that in fact
f\in H^{k}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
. If we set
d=0
in the problem (
122
), then this defines the right inverse
f=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{\dagger}z
, proving the proposition.
∎

Appendix A
Semigroup extension theory for linear collisions

We now provide a proof of Lemma
2.10
. In this section, we impose conditions (
6
) and (
7
) on the collision kernel
B_{s,\gamma}
, but for simplicity of notation, we do not assume (
8
), namely we let
s\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
vary, and we will give more precise conditions when needed. For consistency, we fix an angular collision kernel
b_{s}(\cos\theta)
for each
s\in(0,1)
, which by symmetrization, we can assume to be supported in
\theta\in(0,\pi/2]
. Invertibility of the operators
\underline{L}_{s,\gamma}
in
L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k})
away from the kernel was proved for
s\in(0,1/2)
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
in
[
37
]
, using the semigroup extension theory for non-symmetric operators developed in
[
58
]

[
34
]
, by decomposing the operator into a hypodissipative part and a regularizing part. We prove uniform invertibility of the regularized uncentred operators
L_{\bar{u},\kappa}
by a similar strategy.

We now define the notation we will need in this section. We fix a truncation function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
1_{[-1,1]}\leq\chi\leq 1_{[-2,2]}
, and write
\chi_{\delta}(x)=\chi(x/\delta)
where the regularization parameter
\delta>0
may vary, and define the truncated angular collision kernel

\displaystyle b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)=b_{s}(\cos\theta)\chi_{\delta}(\theta)

and split
b_{s}=b_{s}^{(\delta)}+b_{s}^{(\delta),\mathrm{c}}
. We then similarly split the collision kernel
B_{s,\gamma}
into singular and remainder parts

\displaystyle B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(r,\cos\theta)=r^{\gamma}b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta),\quad B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta),c}(r,\cos\theta)=r^{\gamma}b_{s}^{(\delta),c}(\cos\theta),

and define the corresponding collision operators

\displaystyle Q_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(g,f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}\ [g_{*}^{\prime}f^{\prime}-g_{*}f]\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi

and
Q_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta),c}=Q_{s,\gamma}-Q_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}
. We then define the anisotropic norm

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s,\gamma,*}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)\underline{M}(\xi_{*})\left\langle\xi_{*}\right\rangle^{-\gamma}(f^{\prime}\left\langle\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}-f\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2})^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

for
s\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\in(0,2)
introduced in
[
37
]
. For any weight function
m
, we will also define the weighted norm
\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s,\gamma,*}(m)}=\left\|mf\right\|_{H^{s,\gamma,*}}
.

We have the following trilinear estimate on the collision operator.

Lemma A.1
.

Assume
k>\gamma/2+2+\max(1/2,2s)
and
(s,\gamma)\in(0,1/2)\times[0,2]
. Defining the weight
m=\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k}
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle Q_{s,\gamma}(f,g),h)\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\xi}(m)}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{2s}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma+2s}m)}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(m)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}.

This generalizes Lemma 2.3 of
[
37
]
, which requires
\gamma\in(0,1)
and requires slightly more polynomial moments
k
. The arguments used for
\gamma\in(0,1)
mostly carry through unchanged for the extended range
\gamma\in[0,2]
, so we write the arguments where they differ for the extended range, and refer to
[
37
]
for the rest of the details of the proof.

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

We re-express

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{s,\gamma}(f,g),h\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\xi}(m)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{s,\gamma}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)(f^{\prime}_{*}g^{\prime}-f_{*}g)\,h\,m^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{s,\gamma}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)(f^{\prime}_{*}g^{\prime}m^{\prime}-f_{*}g\,m)\,h\,m\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{s,\gamma}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)f^{\prime}_{*}g^{\prime}h\,m\,(m-m^{\prime})\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

as in the proof of Lemma 2.3 of
[
37
]
, where

\displaystyle\cos\theta=\sigma\cdot\frac{\xi-\xi_{*}}{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|},

and where we write
g^{\prime}
for
g(\xi^{\prime})
, and similarly write
f_{*}^{\prime},f_{*},
and
m^{\prime}
. We note that by Theorem 1.1 of
[
36
]
, we can bound

\displaystyle I_{1}=\left\langle Q_{s,\gamma}(f,gm),hm\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma+2s})}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{2s}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma+2s}m)}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(m)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{2s}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma+2s}m)}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(m)}

for
(s,\gamma)\in(0,1)\times[0,2]
, where the second bound follows from
k>\gamma/2+2+\max(1/2,2s)
. The proof of Lemma 2.3(i) of
[
37
]
then gives the estimate

\displaystyle I_{2}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}

for
(s,\gamma)\in(0,1)\times[0,2]
, which relies on cancellation estimates developed in
[
3
]
to control the angular singularity when
s<1/2
. This concludes the proof of the lemma. The rest of the proof of Lemma 2.3 of
[
37
]
can be followed line by line for the case
\gamma\in[0,2]
.
∎

We use this lemma to prove the following coercivity result.

Lemma A.2
.

For
k>\gamma/2+2+\max(1/2,2s)
and
(s,\gamma)\in(0,1/2)\times[0,2]
, setting the weight
m=\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k}
, we have the coercivity estimate

\displaystyle\left\langle\underline{L}_{s,\gamma}h,h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(m)}\leq-c_{0}\delta^{2-2s}\left\|h\right\|_{H^{s,\gamma,*}(m)}^{2}-c_{0}\delta^{-2s}\left\|h\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}+C_{\delta}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

for any
\delta>0
sufficiently small.

This was proved in Lemma 4.2 of
[
37
]
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
. This proof relies on the entropy dissipation lower bound of
[
2
]
and estimates developed for the grazing-collision limit in
[
35
]
, and we verify that these hold for
s\in(0,1/2)
and
\gamma\in[0,2]
. Before we can prove Lemma
A.2
, we need the following preparatory results.

Lemma A.3

(Corollary 2.1 of
[
2
]
)
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
be non-negative functions, then we have the bound

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)g_{*}(f-f^{\prime})^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{1}{16\pi^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\left|\hat{f}(\zeta)\right|^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}\left(\sigma\cdot\frac{\zeta}{\left|\zeta\right|}\right)\left(\hat{g}(0)-\hat{g}(\zeta^{-})\right)\,{\rm d}\sigma\right)\,{\rm d}\zeta

where
\hat{f}(\zeta)
denotes the Fourier transform of
f(\xi)
in the velocity variable, and
\zeta^{-}=(\zeta-\left|\zeta\right|\sigma)/2
.

To prove Lemma
A.2
, we will need asymptotics for this estimate in
\delta>0
, which we prove in the following lemma.

Lemma A.4
.

For any non-negative function
g\in L^{1}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle)
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}\!\left(\frac{\zeta}{\left|\zeta\right|}\cdot\sigma\right)\left(\hat{g}(0)-\left|\hat{g}(\zeta^{-})\right|\right)\,{\rm d}\sigma\geq\delta^{2-2s}\left(c_{0}\left|\zeta\right|^{2s}-c_{1}\right),

for all
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where the constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
depend on
g
.

Proof.

Using Lemma 3 of
[
2
]
, we have the estimate

(124)

\displaystyle\hat{g}(0)-\left|\hat{g}(\zeta)\right|\geq C_{g}^{\prime}\min(\left|\zeta\right|^{2},1)

for any
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, proved using non-negativity
g\geq 0
.
Using that

\displaystyle\left|\zeta^{-}\right|^{2}=\frac{\left|\zeta\right|^{2}}{2}\left(1-\sigma\cdot\frac{\zeta}{\left|\zeta\right|}\right)

and defining
\cos\vartheta=\sigma\cdot\zeta/\left|\zeta\right|
,
we can bound

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}(\cos\vartheta)\,\min\!\left(\frac{\left|\zeta\right|^{2}}{2}(1-\cos\vartheta),1\right)\chi_{\delta}(\theta)\,{\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle\quad=2\pi\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\!b_{s}(\cos\vartheta)\sin(\vartheta)\min\!\left(\frac{\left|\zeta\right|^{2}}{2}(1-\cos\vartheta),1\right)\chi_{\delta}(\vartheta)\,{\rm d}\vartheta

\displaystyle\quad\geq C\int_{0}^{\delta}\!\vartheta^{-1-2s}\min\left(\left|\zeta\right|^{2}\theta^{2},1\right)\,{\rm d}\vartheta

\displaystyle\quad=C\left|\zeta\right|^{2s}\int_{0}^{\left|\zeta\right|\delta}\!\tilde{\vartheta}^{-1-2s}\min(\tilde{\vartheta}^{2},1)\,{\rm d}\tilde{\vartheta}

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{cases}C^{\prime}\delta^{2-2s}\left|\zeta\right|^{2}&\left|\zeta\right|\leq\delta^{-1}\\
C^{\prime}\delta^{-2s}+C^{\prime\prime}\left|\zeta\right|^{2s}\left(1-\left|\zeta\right|^{-2s}\delta^{-2s}\right)&\left|\zeta\right|>\delta^{-1}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\quad\geq\delta^{2-2s}\left(c_{0}\left|\zeta\right|^{2s}-c_{1}\right)

where we have used the change of variables
\tilde{\vartheta}=\left|\zeta\right|\vartheta
, and combining this with the bound (
124
) proves the claim.
∎

We can now prove coercivity estimates on
\underline{L}_{s,\gamma}
by following arguments from the proof of Lemma 4.2 of
[
37
]
, and showing that they hold for the extended range
s\in(0,1/2)
and
\gamma\in[0,2]
.

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

As in the proof of Lemma 4.2 of
[
37
]
, we define
H=hm
, and compute

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{s,\gamma}(\underline{M},h),h\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\xi}(m)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)\left[\underline{M}_{*}^{\prime}h^{\prime}-\underline{M}_{*}h\right]\,h\,m^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)\left[\underline{M}_{*}^{\prime}H^{\prime}-\underline{M}_{*}H\right]\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)\underline{M}_{*}^{\prime}h^{\prime}hm(m-m^{\prime})\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\left\langle Q_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\underline{M},H),H\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\xi}}+\left\langle Q_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta),\mathrm{c}}(\underline{M},H),H\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\xi}}+R,

and we bound each term separately. The first term can be rewritten, as in Lemma 4.2 of
[
37
]
, as

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\underline{M},H),H\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)\underline{M}_{*}H(H^{\prime}-H)\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)\underline{M}_{*}(H^{\prime}-H)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)\underline{M}_{*}\left((H^{\prime})^{2}-H^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-I_{1}+I_{2}.

We now use the cancellation lemma in
[
2
]
, which gives that for
(s,\gamma)\in(0,1)\times(-3,2]
, we can express

\displaystyle I_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\left(S_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}*H^{2}\right)\underline{M}\,{\rm d}\xi,

where

\displaystyle S_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(z)=2\pi\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\!\sin\theta\left[\frac{1}{\cos(\theta/2)}\,B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}\!\left(\frac{\left|z\right|}{\cos(\theta/2)},\cos\theta\right)-B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\left|z\right|,\cos\theta)\right]\,{\rm d}\theta.

Using
b_{s}(\cos\theta)\sin\theta\sim\theta^{-1-2s}
, we can compute the asymptotics

\displaystyle\sin\theta\left[\frac{1}{\cos(\theta/2)}\,B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}\!\left(\frac{\left|z\right|}{\cos(\theta/2)},\cos\theta\right)-B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\left|z\right|,\cos\theta)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=b_{s}(\cos\theta)\sin(\theta)\chi_{\delta}(\theta)\left|z\right|^{\gamma}\,\Big(\cos^{-1-\gamma}(\theta/2)-1\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\sim\theta^{1-2s}\chi_{\delta}(\theta)\left|z\right|^{\gamma}

and using that
\operatorname{spt}\chi_{\delta}\subset[-2\delta,2\delta]
, we get the asymptotics

\displaystyle S_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(z)\lesssim\delta^{2-2s}\left|z\right|^{\gamma},

and we deduce from this that

(125)

\displaystyle I_{2}\lesssim\delta^{2-2s}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}.

We now bound the
I_{1}
term. Using the bound

\displaystyle\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{\gamma}\geq\varepsilon\left\langle\xi-\xi_{*}\right\rangle^{\gamma}-\varepsilon 1_{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{\gamma}\leq\varepsilon/(1-\varepsilon)}

which holds for
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
and
\gamma\in[0,2]
, we follow the computations in the proof of Lemma 4.2 of
[
37
]
to bound

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\geq C\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)\underline{M}_{*}\left\langle\xi_{*}\right\rangle^{-\gamma}\left(H^{\prime}\left\langle\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}-H\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad-C\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)\underline{M}_{*}\left\langle\xi_{*}\right\rangle^{-\gamma}H^{2}\left(\left\langle\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}-\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)1_{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|^{\gamma}\leq\varepsilon/(1-\varepsilon)}\underline{M}_{*}\left(H^{\prime}-H\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=I_{11}-I_{12}-I_{13}.

By the definition of the
\dot{H}^{s,\gamma,*}_{\xi}
norm, we immediately get

\displaystyle I_{11}\gtrsim\varepsilon\left\|h\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s,\gamma,*}_{\xi}(m)}^{2}.

To treat the second term, we use that the bound

\displaystyle\left(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}-\left\langle\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}\right)^{2}\lesssim\sin^{2}(\theta/2)\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma}\left\langle\xi_{*}\right\rangle^{4-\gamma},

proved in equation (2.7) of
[
37
]
, holds for
\gamma\in[0,2]
, and therefore we can compute

\displaystyle I_{12}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)\sin^{2}(\theta/2)\underline{M}_{*}\left\langle\xi_{*}\right\rangle^{4-2\gamma}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma}H^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi,

but this has angular integral

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\!b_{s}(\cos\theta)\sin(\theta)\sin^{2}(\theta/2)\chi_{\delta}(\theta)\,{\rm d}\theta\sim\delta^{2-2s},

which gives us the bound

\displaystyle I_{12}\lesssim\varepsilon\delta^{2-2s}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}^{2}.

To treat the third term, we note that since
\varepsilon<1/2
, we can bound

\displaystyle I_{13}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)1_{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|\leq 1}\underline{M}_{*}\left(H^{\prime}-H\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}(\cos\theta)1_{\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|\leq 1}\underline{M}_{*}\left(H^{\prime}-H\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

for
\gamma\in[0,2]
(this is trivially true when
\gamma=0
).
But we can then use Lemma
A.6
to bound this by

\displaystyle I_{13}\lesssim\varepsilon\left\|h\right\|_{H^{s}_{\xi}(m)}^{2}

using Lemma
A.6
. Combining these estimates gives us the bound

(126)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I_{1}\geq c_{1}\varepsilon\left\|h\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s,\gamma,*}_{\xi}(m)}^{2}-c_{2}\varepsilon\left\|h\right\|_{H^{s}_{\xi}(\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}m)}^{2}

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
. The negative term is a high order error term which we would like to replace with an
L^{2}
norm, so we compensate (
126
) with a second bound on
I_{1}
. Following arguments from the proof of Theorem 3.1 of
[
35
]
, we decompose

\displaystyle 2I_{1}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)(\underline{M}\chi_{R})_{*}(H^{\prime}-H)^{2}(1-\chi_{3R}(\xi))^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!B_{s,\gamma}^{(\delta)}(\left|\xi-\xi_{*}\right|,\cos\theta)(\underline{M}\chi_{R})_{*}(H^{\prime}-H)^{2}\frac{\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma}}{\left\langle 6R\right\rangle^{\gamma}}\chi_{3R}(\xi)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=I_{11}^{\prime}+I_{12}^{\prime}

for some
R>0
. To bound
I_{12}^{\prime}
, we follow the arguments of
[
2
]
, with more precise control of the angular truncation. We take points
\xi^{(j)}
for
1\leq j\leq J
and define the balls
A_{j}=B(\xi^{(j)},r_{0})
such that

\displaystyle B(0,6R)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{J}A_{j}

for some small
r_{0}>0
, and we define
B_{j}=B(0,R)\setminus B(0,2r_{0})
. We define a smooth partition of unity
(\chi_{A_{j}})_{j=1}^{J}
on
B(0,6R)
, such that
0\leq\chi_{A_{j}}\leq 1
and

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}\chi_{A_{j}}=1\qquad\hbox{on }\ B(0,6R),

and we define smooth cutoff functions
\chi_{B_{j}}
with support in
B_{j}
, and such that

\displaystyle 1_{B(0,R-r_{0})\setminus B(\xi^{(j)},3r_{0})}\leq\chi_{B_{j}}\leq 1_{B_{j}}.

The proof of the truncation lemma of
[
2
]
then immediately gives us that

\displaystyle I_{12}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq C_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!b_{s}^{(\delta)}(\cos\theta)(\underline{M}\chi_{B_{j}})_{*}\left(H^{\prime}\left\langle\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}{\chi_{A_{j}}}^{\prime}-H\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}\chi_{A_{j}}\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}\sigma\,{\rm d}\xi_{*}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\qquad-C_{0}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}^{2}

\displaystyle=C_{0}I_{121}^{\prime}-C_{0}\delta^{2-2s}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(m)}^{2}

where
C_{0}
depends on
R,r_{0}
, and
\gamma
